Dialogs and chat windows can be resized by dragging their edges; double-click an edge to reset that one. A dialog's side panel (the character details, Settings, the Lorebooks manager, the Custom CSS editor, the creator) widens the same way, by its inner edge.

Character details and the grid

Settings > General > Character Details:

  • Show provider tagline in character details (on), with Allow rich tagline display (off) for HTML taglines.
  • Rich creator's notes rendering (on): creator's notes render with their own styling in a sealed frame.
  • Expand creator's notes by default (off).
  • Snap-scroll in browse previews (phones only, off).
  • Collapse all browse preview sections by default (off).

Card Grid & Browse is in The library. Its phone-only settings: Provider quick switch, Hide modal back-arrows (rely on the phone's back button instead), Quick-import button on browse previews, Swipe gestures on character cards and Haptic feedback.

Imports holds Import unrecognized URLs as direct downloads (see Importing and exporting).

Custom CSS

Settings > General > Custom CSS > Open Custom CSS Editor lets you restyle anything with your own CSS. Use it at your own risk: the app's class names can change between versions.

The editor has two modes:

  • Raw: one block of CSS. Apply Raw makes it the active CSS; Save as Snippet keeps it as a snippet.
  • Snippets: named pieces of CSS you switch on and off and drag into order. New adds one; Save Snippet saves it; Apply Snippets combines the switched-on snippets, in order, into the active CSS.
  1. The two modes, Snippets and Raw.
  2. New: adds a snippet.
  3. A snippet's row: drag it into order, tick its box to switch it on, click its name to edit it, and Open AI assistant (shown when you point at the row) to work on it with the CSS Assistant, below.
  4. Deletes the snippet you are editing.

Custom CSS is limited to 64 KB, and script and style tags are removed. Overriding the app's own colour and size tokens (the ones Every token in the theme editor lists) is the most reliable way to restyle it.

If your CSS makes the app unusable, open the browser's developer console and run clResetCSS(): it clears the active CSS and keeps your snippets. clDisableCSS() switches it off for that tab only, until a reload, which tells you quickly whether your CSS is the cause.

The CSS Assistant

The wand on a snippet in the Custom CSS editor (Open AI assistant) opens the CSS Assistant: describe a change to the app's look, and it writes CSS for it, knowing the app's components and tokens. The snippet you opened it from is sent along with your next message, so you can refine it. Each answer's CSS can be copied, saved as a new snippet (switched off until you switch it on), or written over the snippet you started from. It needs a connection; pick it in its Connection menu (see Connections).

  1. Connection and its Overrides.
  2. Reset: starts a new conversation.
  3. Leaves the snippet out of your next message, for a fresh start.
  4. Describe a UI tweak (Enter to send, Shift+Enter for newline): what you want changed.
  5. Send.

Themes, Custom CSS and theme extensions

Three things change how the app looks. They stack, and each suits a different job.

The theme

The theme editor (above) sets the app's design tokens: the named colours, sizes, corners, fonts and effects its stylesheet is built from. It cannot add a rule of its own, use a picture (a value with url( is refused), or reach anything the tokens do not cover. The theme belongs to your account and follows you to every device, and app updates keep it, since the tokens are the app's stable surface; a value for a token an update removes is dropped. Export saves it as a file and Import loads one. The file also carries your active custom CSS; when that differs from yours, Import asks first, and Keep mine (the answer Escape gives) leaves your CSS alone while Take its CSS replaces it once you save. Making a theme takes minutes, with a live preview, and Reset Defaults puts it back.

Custom CSS

Custom CSS takes any CSS: any selector, any property, pictures and fonts from the web included (a picture or font from another site is fetched from there whenever the app is open). Keep it as snippets you switch on and off one by one, or as one block. It belongs to your account like the theme, but has no file of its own: the theme's Export carries the active CSS as one block. Rules that name the app's classes can stop matching when an update changes its markup; rules that only set tokens keep working. A bad snippet can hide the whole interface, which clDisableCSS() and clResetCSS() undo (see Custom CSS). The CSS Assistant writes snippets for you.

Theme extensions

An extension can be a look of its own. The simplest is a stylesheet alone: a folder holding a manifest and a CSS file, installed once on the server and switched on by each account that wants it. A full theme extension like the bundled Midnight theme adds code: a settings section, palettes, layout choices written as classes on the page, a sky drawn behind everything, a tint read from a character's portrait, commands and a menu verb, and files it keeps for your account. Extensions run with full access to the app and are installed by an admin (see Extensions); they are shared as a folder, a zip or a git repository that any Project Σ server can install. Switching one off takes its look away, and starting the server with CL_EXTENSIONS=off takes every extension's away. Making one ranges from a CSS file and a few lines of manifest to a full extension (see Writing extensions).

How they stack

When two rules are equally specific, the later layer wins:

  1. The app's own stylesheets.
  2. The stylesheets of the app's modules and of your extensions, in the order they load.
  3. Your theme: the theme editor's tokens and your accent.
  4. Your custom CSS, always last.

So a token your custom CSS sets beats the same token in the theme (the theme editor then marks that setting Set in CSS, and moving it changes nothing on screen), and both beat an extension's stylesheet. A more specific rule, or one marked !important, still wins whatever its layer. The palette accent of Midnight or Σ is such a rule: it applies only while you have picked no accent of your own and your custom CSS sets none. Accent Color shows a warning when your custom CSS overrides the accent.

A snippet that is almost a theme

A snippet that only sets tokens on :root, such as :root { --accent: #e0a040; }, looks like a theme and mostly behaves like one. The differences:

  • It outranks the theme editor, which shows Set in CSS on every setting the snippet covers.
  • It is not part of the theme, so the theme editor's presets and Reset Defaults leave it alone.
  • It travels in a theme file only inside the active CSS block, and an import replaces the receiver's active CSS rather than joining their snippets.
  • It can do what a theme cannot (a url(, any selector), and nothing checks its values.

Which to use

You wantUse
Different colours, corners, spacing or fonts across the appThe theme
To hand your whole look to someone as one fileThe theme's Export, which brings your active custom CSS along
To restyle one part, hide something, or use a pictureA Custom CSS snippet
Several tweaks you switch on and off separatelySnippets
A tweak written for you from a descriptionThe CSS Assistant
One look for several accounts, or for other serversA theme extension that is a stylesheet
A look with its own settings, palettes or motionA full theme extension, like Midnight or Σ

Developer

Settings > Developer:

  • Logging > Enable debug logging: extra detail in the browser's console and the server's log.
  • Character Inspection > Show "Info" tab in character modal: adds the Info tab to character details (see Character details).
  • Export > Export Character downloads: Card PNG (the default) or CHARX (Card and Gallery), the card as a V3 card with its picture and every gallery file (see Exporting one character).
  • Export > Export as provider links: makes Export Character copy the character's online link instead of downloading a PNG (see Importing and exporting).

Info

Settings > Info shows the version of Project Σ the server runs (Version Info) and, under Repository, where the project's public repository is.

Extensions

Extensions add features to Project Σ: windows in a chat, actions in menus, views beside Characters and Chats, online sites, macros, connection kinds, themes. A set ships with the app, and others can be installed.

Installed extensions run with full access to the app, the server and the computer it runs on. Read Before you install anything first.

Before you install anything

Read this before installing any extension that did not ship with the app.

An installed extension is code that runs inside Project Σ with no limits. There is no permission system and no sandbox, and nothing in the app can stop an extension that wants to do harm. Once installed, an extension can:

  • read, change or delete every character, chat, lorebook, picture and setting on the server, for every account;
  • see the API keys, passwords and logins saved in Project Σ, and act on the sites the Hosted Browser is signed in to;
  • read and write files on the computer running the server, and reach anything that computer can reach, on your network and on the internet.

If it has a server part, that part starts at the next server restart, whether or not anyone switches it on, and runs on the server with access to every account. Switching it on for your account runs its code for you, in your browser, on the server or both, with everything your account can do.

Project Σ does not review, check or vouch for extensions, and is not responsible for what they do. Whatever an extension does is on its author and on whoever installed it. So:

  • install only an extension whose code you have read, or one from an author you have real reason to trust, and read the code even then: accounts get taken over and projects change hands;
  • prefer an extension whose source sits in a public repository with a history you can look at over a zip from a stranger;
  • treat every update as new code, and look at what changed before you update;
  • on a server other people use, remember that what you install runs for all of them;
  • when in doubt, do not install it.

The extensions in the Bundled group ship with Project Σ and update with it; everything above is about the ones you install.

Turning extensions on

Settings > Extensions lists them in two groups: Installed (extensions added to this server) and Bundled (the set that ships with the app). The switch on each row turns it on or off for your account only; it takes effect at once, without a reload, and follows you to your other devices.

Open a row (the chevron) to read what the extension adds and to reach its own settings, which appear there while it is on. Extensions that call a model have a Connection setting. Its default is The one the chat uses for an extension that works on a chat (the chat's own connection if you gave it one, else the default) and The one your chats use for one that does not (see Connections).

The bundled extensions

Every bundled extension starts switched off.

They are examples, not core features. Every bundled extension was vibe-coded: written entirely by AI, without the author reviewing it line by line. They were built as a stress test of the extension API, an attempt at what someone other than the author might build on it. Nothing else in the app depends on them, and they may have rough edges: use them as they are if they suit you, or as samples to learn from or to start an extension of your own.

Midnight theme

The Midnight theme gives the whole app the look of an observatory at night, and every part of it can be set back to a plainer form. Switch it on in its row under Settings > Extensions, then open the row; its settings apply as you change them.

  • Palette: Midnight (ice blue on navy), Aurora (teal into violet), Ember (amber and rose), Moonlit (silver, high contrast), or Auto, which follows the hour: Ember at dawn, Moonlit by day, Aurora at dusk and Midnight at night. New Palette opens an editor for one of your own (its Accent, Base hue, Glow and Sky), with the app showing it as you go; Save Palette keeps it for your account. An accent colour you picked in the theme or in custom CSS stays, and the palette colours the rest.
  • Layout: Navigation (Left rail or Top bar) and Top bar (Floating capsule or Docked), both for a desktop; Grid tiles (Windows, each portrait in a window with its name on a plate, or Full-bleed); Window shape (Arch, Rounded or Square); Names on tiles (Always or As the app shows them); Conversation (Editorial, replies set under their speaker's name and your turns in glass capsules, Glass bubbles, or The app's own plates); Character details (Hero banner, opening on a band of the portrait's light, or Plain header).
  • Type and shape: Display face (Marcellus, System serif or The app's own) and Corners (Soft or Crisp).
  • Motion and sky: Motion (Full, with shooting stars, Gentle, at half speed, or Off; it stays still when your system asks for less motion) and Hover lift. Draw the sky puts stars, an aurora, embers or a moonlit haze behind the app, as the palette says, and How much sky sets how much of it there is (Balanced, Sparse or Rich). Tint a conversation with its character's colours leans each conversation's glow toward its character's portrait.

Next Palette in the More Options menu (the theme-midnight.next command) steps to the next palette, your own and Auto included. Your own theme settings and custom CSS still apply over Midnight.

Σ theme

The Σ theme is the app's own design, finished. The quiet look of the chat windows (Inter, thin edges lit along the top, soft depth) is carried through every surface, the colours are calm, and a slow drift of light sits behind it all. Every part of it can be set back to a plainer form. Switch it on in its row under Settings > Extensions, then open the row; its settings apply as you change them. Σ and Midnight each restyle the whole app, so keep one of them on at a time.

  • Palette: Σ (blue and indigo), Iris (violet and rose), Lagoon (teal and blue), Amber (amber and coral) or Graphite (high contrast). New Palette opens an editor for one of your own (its two Colours, its Background hue and its Glow), with the app showing it as you go; Save Palette keeps it for your account, and Edit reopens the one in use. An accent colour you picked in the theme or in custom CSS stays, and the palette colours the rest.
  • Layout: Navigation (Top bar, or Left rail, a slim rail of icons down the left edge; for a desktop, and a window narrower than 1000 pixels keeps the bar); Grid tiles (Σ tiles, each portrait with its name and tags on its lower edge, or The app's own); Names on tiles (Always or As the app shows them); Conversation (Cards, each message on a card of its own with yours in the accent's tint, Open, replies set on the page and your turns in bubbles at the right, or The app's own plates); Character details (Hero banner, opening under a banner lit by the portrait with the name set large, or Plain header).
  • Type and shape: Typeface (Inter, the face the chat windows already use, or The app's own) and Corners (Rounded or Tight).
  • Motion and field: Motion (Full, Gentle, at half speed, or Off; it stays still when your system asks for less motion) and Hover lift. Draw the field puts soft pools of the palette's light behind the app, drifting slowly. Tint a conversation with its character's colours leans each conversation's accent and glow toward its character's portrait.

Search everything wears Σ's material while Σ is on. Next Σ Palette in the More Options menu (the theme-sigma.next command) steps to the next palette, your own included. Your own theme settings and custom CSS still apply over Σ.

Getting more from the bundled set

  • Chat Timeline: open it from a chat's Timeline window, or from Chat Timeline in a character's card menu or a chat's menu in the Chats view; that dialog resizes from its edges and keeps its size. It draws every chat with the character as one tree: each card is a stretch of messages, showing how that stretch begins, and the highlighted line is the chat you have open (or the latest one). Drag to move, scroll or pinch to zoom, and Fit or Your chat brings you back.
    • Chats lists every chat with how many messages it holds and how long ago it was used: beside the map when the window is wide, otherwise over its foot. Pick one to light its path, or its arrow to open it. A chat you named is shown by its name; one with the name it started with is shown by its date and time (and its branch number), the full name when you point at it.
    • Click a card to read that moment. It is headed by the chat you read it in, the one you have open when it passes through; the other chats that share it are listed under it, and picking one reads the moment there instead. Then come how the stretch starts and ends. Open opens that chat at this point and Branch from Here starts a new chat from it. Double-click a card to open it too. A card several chats share shows how many.
    • Every chat that ends has a flag naming it: after its last card, or under a card the other chats carry on from. Click a flag to open that chat; when three or more end at one card, a More tab shows them all.
    • The search finds words in every chat with the character. Enter steps through the matches, each shown with the line around it.
    • Swipes draws each reply's unused swipes as faded branches, and Take This Road starts a new chat that takes one of them.
    • Style picks the map's look: App (follows your theme), Parchment, Blueprint, Nebula or Daylight. Press Your Own to start your own style from the one on show, or just change a colour, the background, the corners or the type; Export saves it to a file and Import reads one back. Map style in its settings picks one too.
  • Memory: with Update after this many new messages at 0, the summary updates only when you ask, in the chat's Memory window.
  • Trackers: with Update after every reply off, a tracker updates only when you press Update Now in its window; on, each reply costs one model call per tracker the chat keeps. In the tracker editor, each field says how often it changes: Updated every reply, Filled once, then kept (the model writes it the first time and leaves it), or Yours: never updated (a field you fill in yourself, right in the tracker's window).
  • Translator: Translate into is only the default; a chat can choose its own language in its Translator window.
  • Story Beats: drag a beat by its grip to move it within its group. Its settings: The director (whether your model reads the chat every few replies to start and finish beats, and how it paces the story) and In the prompt (how the active beats are sent and how much room they may take).
  • Next Beat: each button asks for a different kind of move. Crossroads gives a spread, every suggestion a different kind of turn; Raise the Stakes, Quiet Beat, Reveal, Knock at the Door (someone or something arrives) and Jump Ahead (on to the next moment worth playing) each lean one way; Genres and Your Styles open menus of more. After Dark, for adult fiction, appears once you switch it on in the settings. Surprise Me arms a hidden turn the story brings in a few messages later, without telling you what it is. Director plays a scene you sketch several ways (Takes on one scene) or gives a suggestion for each step you list (Steps in order). Its settings come in three groups: Suggestions (how many, from 1 to 8; how long, from Brief to Detailed; and how much of the chat they read), Insert and Send (whether a suggestion replaces what the message box holds or adds a line under it, and how it is wrapped) and Surprise Me (the range of messages after which a surprise lands, the same number at both ends landing it at exactly that distance, and Endless, which arms the next from the same style each time one lands). On a phone its window scrolls as one page, the suggestions under its buttons, and a short scroll up brings the buttons back over them.
  • Ratings and Notes: Rate the Selection in the selection toolbar rates every selected character at once.
  • Lore Search: Search Lore in More Options and in a chat's options menu searches every lorebook, and More Like This on a lorebook entry finds entries like it.

The Chat Timeline, opened from a character's card menu:

  1. Swipes: draws each reply's unused swipes as faded branches.
  2. Every message: draws each message as its own card, instead of one card per stretch.
  3. Fit, Your chat and Reload.
  4. Style: the map's look.
  5. Chats: the list of chats beside the map.
  6. The zoom: Zoom out, the level (click it to go back to 100%) and Zoom in.
  7. Opens that chat.

The Story Beats window, with three beats planned:

  1. Drag a beat by its grip to move it within its group.
  2. A beat's title: opens it to edit.
  3. A beat's Start, Skip and Delete, which show brighter when you point at them.
  4. Add Beat.

The Trackers window, keeping the Scene tracker, and its editor:

  1. A tracker the chat keeps; click its tab to show it.
  2. Keep track of something else: adds another tracker to the chat.
  3. A field that is yours to fill in.
  4. Edit: the tracker editor, below.
  5. Update Now.
  1. A field: its key, its label, its kind, and how often it changes.
  2. How the field shows in the window (Headline, Badge or Detail), its tone, and an example of what it holds.
  3. Move up, Move down and Remove.

An extension whose connection was deleted goes back to its default (the one the chat uses, or the one your chats use) and says so in its settings.

Installing an extension

Settings > Extensions opens on a reminder, for everyone who can switch an extension on, that installed extensions run with full access. Installing is for admins (on a single-user install, you). Under it, the install group offers:

  • a git repository address and Install (plain https addresses; the server needs git);
  • Install from a File: a .zip, such as a repository's "Download ZIP";
  • or copy the extension's folder into extensions/installed/ on the server and refresh the list.

Every install first shows what an extension can do and asks you to switch on "I understand the risk" before Install works. A new extension is installed switched off: switching an installed extension on for your account says once more what it will be able to do, and asks.

Remove from the server deletes an installed extension, its files and its folder in every account's home; if an account's folder cannot be deleted, the extension is still removed and a warning names those accounts. The tables it kept in each account's library stay, with their rows, until you drop them: npm run verify lists them as left over, and npm run verify -- --drop-leftover <id> drops them after a backup (see "Data and backups"). Bundled extensions cannot be removed or updated, only switched off.

Keeping installed extensions up to date

An extension installed from a git address updates in place; one installed another way is updated by replacing its folder.

  • Updates, under Install, says when the extensions were last checked and what that check found: every one up to date, which ones have an update waiting, or which could not be checked. Check Now on it asks each installed extension's repository whether it has a newer version. It updates nothing: an extension with one shows update available in its row, with the version coming beside the one you have.
  • The update button on an extension's row asks you to confirm, since an update is new code, then brings it to the newest version its repository holds, even when the author rewrote the repository's history. On a phone it sits under the extension's description and names the version coming. Changes made by hand inside the extension's folder are replaced. A new version that would not load is refused, and the extension stays as it was. The new version runs at once in the tab you updated from; other open tabs use it after a reload, and code that runs on the server picks it up after a server restart.
  • Tell me when an extension has an update (off by default, and saved the moment you flip it) checks once a day while the app is open and lists the extensions with a newer version in the notifications, with Open Extensions to go to the first of them and update it. On a phone the More button wears a dot while one waits. Nothing is ever updated for you. Off, nothing asks the repositories unless you press Check Now.

When an extension misbehaves

Switch it off in Settings > Extensions. If the app will not load far enough for that, start the server with the environment variable CL_EXTENSIONS=off: the app runs with every extension dark (the Extensions section then says Extensions are switched off). Remove or replace the broken extension's folder in extensions/installed/, then restart without the variable.

Writing your own

The extension authors' guide covers the extension API: events, storage, settings, windows, views, menu actions, macros, prompt blocks and online sites.

Data and backups

Where your data lives

Everything is in the data/ folder next to the install (or wherever the CL_DATA_DIR environment variable points):

  • server.yaml: the server's settings (see the README).
  • server.db: the accounts and the server's own records.
  • One folder per account (data/default/ without accounts), holding:
    • library.db: the whole library (characters, tags, chats, lorebooks, personas, playlists, version history, settings, connections), with its library.db-wal and library.db-shm companion files;
    • avatars/, gallery/, backgrounds/ and personas/: the pictures and media;
    • thumbs/: thumbnails, which are made again on demand if deleted;
    • backups/: the database backups (below).

Installed extensions live in extensions/installed/ (or CL_EXTENSIONS_DIR), outside data/; back them up too if you have any.

Library Backup

Settings > General > Library Backup shows your library's size and counts, when it was last backed up, and the backup controls:

  • Back Up Now copies the library's database into the account's backups/ folder. The copy runs in the background, so you can keep working while it does: the copy is your library as it was when it started, and anything you change meanwhile goes into the next one. Each copy is checked before it is kept. While it runs, the button turns into Cancel, and the notifications show its progress with a cancel of their own; a cancelled backup leaves nothing behind.
  • Automatic backup: Off (the default), Daily or Weekly, keeping the newest few (Keep, 5 by default; older ones are deleted).
  • Quiet hours: automatic backups start only between these hours (03:00 to 06:00 by default), on the server's clock. Pick the same hour twice for any time. The line under them says when the next automatic backup will run.
  • Existing backups lists the copies on disk with their dates and sizes.

If the app was not running during the quiet hours, or the backup there failed or was cancelled, the notifications ask what to do: Back Up Now, Tonight (wait for the next quiet hours) or Skip (the next backup comes on its usual day). One backup runs at a time on a server, so with several accounts one may wait for another to finish.

A backup copies the database, not the images. It protects your characters, chats and everything else stored in the library, but the pictures, galleries and backgrounds are files beside it: copy the whole data/ folder now and then too.

Restoring a backup

There is no restore button; it is done by hand, and step three is the one that matters (the same steps are under How to restore one in the Library Backup group):

  1. Stop the server. Copying over a database that is open damages it.
  2. In the account's folder, rename library.db, and keep it as your way back.
  3. Copy the backup in, rename it to library.db, and delete library.db-wal and library.db-shm if they are there.
  4. Start the server and check that your characters are there.

Images are not part of the backup, so characters imported since the backup keep their files but are missing from the library, and characters deleted since come back without their pictures. npm run verify (below) lists missing files.

Search Index

Settings > General > Search Index shows how quick your searches are, index by index, and lets you tidy them yourself:

  • The three figures (Card text, Chat messages, Lorebook entries) say how many times slower a search can be, when it has to read from the disk, than it would be on a freshly tidied index. 1.0× is as fast as it gets.
  • The line under them says whether the index is in shape, when it was last tidied and how long that took, or, while a tidy-up runs, which index it is on and how far along. If a tidy-up stopped before finishing, it says so and why. Edits saved while a tidy-up runs add new pieces, so after a busy stretch an index can read in pieces again even though the line says it was just tidied.
  • Tidy Now tidies every index that is in pieces, right away and in the background, and keeps going while you work; searches may be a little slower and the disk busier until it ends. On a library of tens of thousands of cards on a hard drive the card text can take several minutes, and a large chat history a minute or so; lorebooks take seconds. Pressing it while the app is tidying on its own takes over and tidies everything. The button rests while every index is already in one piece, and while a tidy-up you asked for runs or waits its turn.
  • Tidy the search index on its own (on by default): everyday edits leave an index in pieces, so once one reaches 2.0× it is tidied for you the next time the app sits idle for two minutes. The moment you click, type or scroll again, the tidy-up pauses and picks up where it left off at the next quiet moment, so the long work happens while you are away and never while you are working. Opening the notifications to see it does not count, and neither does coming back to the app, so you can read what it is doing without stopping it. A tidy-up that stopped before finishing is tried again at the next quiet moment.
  • Load the library into memory at startup (on by default): a few seconds after the server starts, it reads your library once in the background, so the first search after a restart is as quick as every search after it. On a hard drive with a library of 17,000 cards, the first search went from about seven seconds to well under one. The read takes some seconds of disk activity after each start (about twelve on that drive), and the app stays usable throughout. Your system lends it memory it is not using and takes it back whenever another program needs it. A library too large for a quarter of the free memory is left out, and the change applies from the next start.
  • Each index lists every index with how many pieces it is in and its size on disk.

A tidy-up never runs while the library is being backed up: the backup goes first. A backup that starts during a tidy-up pauses it, and one you asked for with Tidy Now carries on once the backup is done. Pressing Tidy Now during a backup does the same: the line says it is waiting for the backup, and the tidy-up starts when it ends.

Copying the data folder

The simplest full backup is a copy of the whole data/ folder made while the server is stopped. It carries every account, every library, every picture and the server's settings. Restoring is copying it back.

Moving to another machine

  • Everything: stop the server, copy data/ (and extensions/installed/ if you use installed extensions) to the new install, and start it there.
  • A selection of characters, with their chats, galleries and lorebooks: export a full bundle and import it on the other install (see Importing and exporting).

To keep the data somewhere other than the install folder, set the CL_DATA_DIR environment variable to that folder.

Checking a library

From a terminal in the install folder:

npm run verify

checks every library in data/ against the rules the server keeps: the database's own integrity, its links between records, its search indexes and counts, and that every picture, gallery file and background its records point at is on disk. It prints what it finds and exits non-zero when anything fails. npm run verify -- --user <handle> checks one account; --db-only skips the file check, the slow part on a large media folder.

It also says who owns each table, index and trigger in a library: the app (as this version builds it), each extension by name (installed, or left over when its extension has left the server), or nobody. A table of the app's that is missing or changed, and anything nobody owns, counts as a failure. An extension's leftover tables are dropped on your word: npm run verify -- --drop-leftover <id> lists them, asks, takes a backup into backups/ as Back Up Now does, then drops them (--yes answers the question for a script). Run it with the server stopped.

A few checks can also repair what they find. npm run verify -- --repair chatMessageObjects removes chat lines an older import stored that were never messages (a line holding only a number, a word or a list), after taking a backup into backups/ as Back Up Now does. Run it with the server stopped. Asked to repair a check that has no repair, it names the ones that do.

Upgrading

A new version upgrades each library's database the first time it opens it; there is no step to run. Take a backup first if you want a way back: a library opened by a newer version cannot be opened by an older one, which refuses it with a message asking you to update.

Bringing a SillyTavern library

Project Σ can bring a whole SillyTavern user folder across: the folder holding characters/, chats/ and worlds/, usually data/default-user inside the SillyTavern install. The SillyTavern folder is only ever read, never changed, and the migration checks its own work against it when it finishes.

What comes across

  • Character cards, with their tags and favorites.
  • Chats, matched to their characters by folder.
  • Every lorebook, which characters each is linked to (including extra lorebooks per character), and the lorebooks SillyTavern had switched on globally.
  • Gallery images and media. Characters that share a name may come across with duplicated galleries; see Before you start.
  • From the Character Library extension, if you used it: version snapshots, playlists, custom CSS, saved chat prompts and filter presets. Saved prompts, CSS snippets and filter presets replace any of the same kind already in your library.
  • Personas: name, description, picture and one bound lorebook each; SillyTavern's default persona becomes your global one.

What stays behind

  • Group chats and group definitions. They are counted and listed so nothing disappears silently, but they stay at the source.
  • The Character Library extension's settings (logins, view preferences and its other options): set up fresh here.
  • SillyTavern's own settings: API connections, sampler presets, themes, Quick Replies, instruct and context templates. Chat-completion prompt presets can be imported separately in the prompt manager.
  • Your own regex scripts, the ones SillyTavern runs in every chat: they live in its settings. Export them from SillyTavern's Regex panel and import them in the Regex scripts window. Scripts saved on a card come across with the card, and a preset's come with the preset when you import it; both stay off until you allow them.
  • Tags applied through SillyTavern's tag manager, which live in its settings rather than on the cards. Tags written in the cards themselves come across.
  • Chat backgrounds, expression sprites and SillyTavern's other asset folders.
  • Card files with no character data inside, and chat folders that match no card (both are counted in the report).

Before you start: characters that share a name

This step is optional. Skipping it loses nothing, but it is worth knowing about.

SillyTavern keeps each character's gallery in a folder named after the character, so characters with the same name (two versions of one card, or two different characters who happen to share a name) share one folder. Project Σ gives every character its own gallery, and nothing in a shared folder says which character an image belongs to, so each character of that name comes across with a full copy of the folder: the same gallery, duplicated, mixing everyone's images.

To avoid that, sort the shared folders with the Character Library extension before you migrate:

  1. In the extension, open "Settings" > "Gallery Folders" and turn on "Use unique gallery folder names". This writes an ID into each card, so the extension asks you to back up your characters folder first.
  2. Press "Assign Gallery IDs" so every character has one.
  3. Press "Smart Relocate" under "Shared Names". It builds a fingerprint for each character from the gallery on the site it came from and the images embedded in its card, matches the shared folder's images against those fingerprints, and moves each match into its owner's own folder. "Migrate All", beside it, is optional here: a name only one character uses comes across to the right character either way.

The migration then reads each character's own folder through the ID in its card. Images Smart Relocate could not match stay in the shared folder, and every character of that name still gets a copy of them.

Plain SillyTavern has no per-character folders, so without the extension, characters that share a name come across with duplicated galleries. Installing the extension for this one step keeps them apart.

The wizard

Settings > Account > Bring your SillyTavern library runs the migration from the app, into your own library. The Coming from the Character Library extension? note above the steps sums up Before you start.

  1. Point at the SillyTavern user folder: type the folder's path, as it is on the machine running the server, and press Check. The plan shows how many cards, chat folders, lorebooks, media files and personas it found, how many characters are new and how many you already have, and any duplicate groups.
  2. Choose what comes across (optional): Skip cards tagged leaves out cards with certain tags; Test pool migrates only some cards first (by name, or a random number of them) so you can look around before the full run; Generate thumbnails as part of the run prepares the grid's thumbnails in the same go.
  3. Run the Migration: the log follows the run, which ends with a check of everything it brought across. Closing the tab does not stop it. Run it as often as you like: every character heals in place and nothing duplicates.
  4. Finalize when you are done: one last check, then the records that link migrated characters to their SillyTavern files are removed, so they behave exactly like characters created here. This is one-way: after it, running the migration into this library again would duplicate everything.

What does not come across under the wizard repeats the list above. Only one migration runs on a server at a time.

The command line

The same tool runs from a terminal in the install folder:

npm run migrate -- <path-to-st-user-dir> --user <handle> [options]

A sensible sequence:

  1. Dry run first, which prints the plan and writes nothing: --dry-run.
  2. Optionally a small test pool to try out in the app: --only "Some Character" --only "Another One", or --sample 20.
  3. The real run: no options. It prints progress, writes a JSON report into the account's folder, and verifies the result.
  4. Finalize when satisfied: --finalize.
OptionWhat it does
--user <handle>The account whose library to migrate into (required); default without accounts
--dry-runPrints the plan and writes nothing
--only <name or file>Migrates only this card, by name or file name; repeat it for more
--sample NMigrates a random N of the cards
--exclude-tag <tag>Leaves out cards with this tag; repeat it for more
--warm-thumbsGenerates the thumbnails as part of the run
--verify full, fast or offHow the run checks itself (below)
--no-verifyThe same as --verify off
--finalizeOne last check, then removes the migration's identity records
--forceRuns into a library that was already finalized (this duplicates everything)
--report <path>Where to write the JSON report

When it finishes it re-reads the source and compares it with your library, field by field, message by message, gallery file by gallery file. The full comparison re-reads every gallery file's bytes, which takes hours on a large library, so once a full check has passed over the whole library, later runs and the finalize use a quick check (everything but re-reading gallery bytes). --verify full forces the full one.

Exit codes: 0 when all went well, 1 for a usage or fatal error, 2 when some items failed to import, 3 when the check found a difference (or finalize refused). The report says exactly where.

With accounts on, create the account with the same handle from the admin panel and it picks the migrated library up.

Duplicates in the source

Two files that carry the same identity (a card duplicated inside SillyTavern, or the same character imported twice from a site) come across as two independent characters, and the plan lists every such group, so nothing merges or vanishes silently. Use Find Duplicates afterwards if you want to tidy them.

After migrating

The first browse of a big freshly migrated library is slow while thumbnails are made. Pass --warm-thumbs, tick Generate thumbnails as part of the run, or use Settings > Media > Bulk Operations > Generate Missing Thumbnails to make them ahead of time.

Then add a connection to chat (see Connections), and import your SillyTavern chat-completion preset in the prompt manager if you had one.

Accounts and server settings

Single-user and multi-user

Out of the box Project Σ has one library and no sign-in: whoever opens the page is the owner, with full access, including installing extensions. That suits one person on their own machine or home network.

With accounts on, everyone signs in, and each account gets its own library, media, settings, connections and personas, in its own folder under data/. Nothing is shared between accounts except the server itself and its installed extensions (each account switches extensions on for itself).

Turning accounts on

Set multiUser: true in data/server.yaml (or start the server with CL_MULTI_USER=true) and restart. On the first start with no accounts, the server creates an admin account called default, which takes over the existing single-user library, and prints its password once in the server's output. Sign in with it; you are asked to choose your own password straight away.

Turning accounts off again makes every visitor default once more; the other accounts' folders stay on disk untouched.

Signing in

With accounts on, the app opens on a sign-in screen. A sign-in lasts 30 days by default (sessionTimeoutDays in server.yaml; -1 for no limit, though a browser keeps a sign-in at most 400 days). Repeated wrong passwords lock that account's sign-in for a while, growing with each further attempt.

Settings > Account shows who you are signed in as, with Change Password and Sign Out. Changing your password signs you out everywhere else. Sign Out signs out this browser only.

Managing accounts

Admins see Users under Settings > Account, listing every account with its badges (admin, disabled, password pending). For each:

  • Issue a temporary password: sets a new one-time password and shows it to you; the person must change it when they next sign in. This is how a forgotten password is reset.
  • Make admin / Remove admin.
  • Disable account / Enable account: a disabled account cannot sign in.
  • Delete account (keeps their data folder): the account goes, its library stays on disk; creating an account with the same handle later picks that library up again.

Add an account takes a handle (lowercase letters, digits and hyphens), an optional display name and a temporary password, with Administrator for an admin. New accounts choose their own password at first sign-in.

The server will not let you remove the last admin, demote or disable yourself, or delete your own account.

Server settings

Server-wide settings (the port, the address it listens on, accounts, cross-origin access, extra host names, whether the launchers open the browser) live in data/server.yaml, written with comments on the first start; most have an environment variable that wins over the file. The full table is in the README. The server refuses to start on an unknown setting or a value of the wrong kind, naming it, rather than run configured differently from what you meant.

Reaching the server from other devices

The server listens on every network interface by default, so phones and other computers on your network can open it by this machine's address. To keep it to this machine alone, set host: 127.0.0.1.

Without accounts, the server answers only requests addressed to an IP address or localhost, so a web page elsewhere cannot point a name of its own at your server. If you reach it by a machine name (a local hostname, or a name on a private network service), add that name to hosts in server.yaml; otherwise you get a page saying the server does not answer to that name. Pages from other sites can never write to your library unless their origin is listed in cors.

Anyone who can reach the port of a server without accounts can do anything there. Keep such a server off networks you do not trust, or turn accounts on.

The Account section

Settings > Account appears on every install: without accounts it holds the SillyTavern migration wizard (see Bringing a SillyTavern library); with accounts, also Signed In (who you are, Change Password and Sign Out) and, for admins, Users.

The API

The server's HTTP API is for power users who want to automate tasks and script their own library, and for extension creators: everything the app does, it does through this same API. Some of what it is good for:

  • bulk tagging and cleanup, or any batch job that would be tedious by hand;
  • scheduled imports and exports, and backups on a timer;
  • reports and statistics on what your library holds;
  • keeping a script or a home automation in step with your library through the change feed.

This page is the orientation; the API reference lists every endpoint with its parameters and answers, and the server publishes the same contract as an OpenAPI spec at GET /api/openapi.json.

The basics

  • Every endpoint is under /api and speaks JSON. Errors answer with a status and { "error": "..." }.
  • GET /api/health answers without signing in: the server's name, version, API version and whether accounts are on.
  • The reference in docs/api-docs.html is generated from the server's own spec; npm run docs regenerates it.

The spec groups the endpoints by area: characters (cards, pictures, import and export), media, backgrounds, chats, the assistant, lorebooks (worlds), the library (playlists, versions, settings, stored documents, backups, bundles, migration, background jobs), tags, the creator studio, LLM connections and calls, extensions, accounts and the admin area, and the infrastructure (health, thumbnails, the change feed).

Signing in

Without accounts, there is no authentication: every request acts as the owner.

With accounts on, POST /api/auth/login with { "handle": "...", "password": "..." } sets a session cookie that later requests send back. GET /api/auth/me says who you are, POST /api/auth/logout signs out, and POST /api/auth/password changes the password. An account that must change its password is refused everything else until it does. There are no separate API keys.

Calling from another site

A page served from another origin may call the API only when that origin is listed in cors in server.yaml (or CL_CORS); requests that carry no origin, such as scripts and command-line tools, are not affected. Writes from a page on another site that is not listed are refused.

The session cookie is restricted to the server's own site, so a page hosted elsewhere works with a server without accounts. The app itself can be opened against another Project Σ server with ?server=<address>; it asks before switching, and remembers the choice on that device.

The change feed

/api/feed is a WebSocket that tells your script what changed, so it can stay in step with the app's tabs, other devices and other scripts. After connecting it sends a hello, then batches of change records naming what changed (a character, a chat, a message, a lorebook, a setting, and so on) and, when a connection falls too far behind, a request to re-read everything. There is no history: after a reconnect, read again what you hold.

Pick an id for your script (8 to 64 letters, digits or hyphens), connect with /api/feed?client=<id>, and send the same id as an X-CL-Client header with your writes: the change records of your own writes then carry it as their origin, so you can skip them.

Background jobs

Changes over large sets (deleting, favoriting or tagging every character a filter matches, adding them to a playlist) answer at once with a job, run on the server a chunk at a time. GET /api/jobs/{id} reports its progress and DELETE /api/jobs/{id} stops it; what already landed stays.

Extensions

Extensions can add endpoints of their own under /api/ext/<id>/. The extension authors' guide covers writing them.

Extensions

Project Σ supports extensions: packages of client and server code that add to or change what the app does. An installed extension is trusted code with the same reach the app itself has, and the install decision is the security boundary.

The extensions bundled with the app were vibe-coded, written entirely by AI as a stress test of this API, without the author reviewing them line by line: an attempt at what someone other than the author might build on it. Read them to see how a door is used in a real extension, not as reviewed reference code. Hello World is the deliberate tour of each door.

The trust model, up front

Installing an extension hands its code full privileges in every user's browser session on this server, and full server-process privileges if it ships a server half: every account's library, the keys and logins saved on the server, the hosted browser's signed-in sites, the files on the machine and everything that machine can reach. A server half starts at the next restart and runs for every account, whether or not anyone switches it on. There is no sandbox and no permission system, by design, and the app cannot protect anyone from an extension that means harm. Project Σ does not review extensions and is not responsible for what they do. So:

  • Only admins can install, update, or remove extensions.
  • Install only code you have read, or code from an author you have real reason to trust, and read it even then: accounts get taken over and projects change hands. Treat every update as new code.
  • The app says so before every install (with an "I understand the risk" switch that holds the Install button), before every update, and when an account switches an installed extension on.
  • Each user chooses which installed extensions run for their own account (Settings > Extensions).
  • CL_EXTENSIONS=off in the server environment boots with the whole extension system dark: nothing is listed, served or loaded. Use it to recover from a broken install without touching the filesystem. on (or leaving it unset) runs them; any other value stops the boot with a message naming the variable.
  • On a single-user server every request acts as the admin, so whoever can reach the server's port can install an extension (as they can do anything else there). The default bind address is every interface; set host in the server config (or CL_HOST) to narrow it.

What this asks of you as an author. Your users cannot be protected by the app, so they are trusting you, and everyone who can push to your repository:

  • Say plainly in your README what your extension reads, what it writes, what it sends and where. Never send anything off the machine without saying so, and never collect data you do not need.
  • Publish your source in a repository with its history, so people can read what they install and what each update changes. A zip with nothing behind it asks for blind trust.
  • Secure the accounts that can publish your extension (two-factor sign-in on your git host): a taken-over account turns every user's next update into an attack.
  • Keep what you reach to what you need: a hosted-browser consumer under the narrowest prefix that works, the stores you use and no others.

Working with the app

Most extensions are written with a coding assistant. The advice below comes from what has actually broken extensions: an update, a second account or another extension tripping over a shortcut. It's worth handing this chapter to your assistant before it plans anything. The extension kit checks the data advice on every test run (see "Testing your extension").

Where the app ends and your extension begins

We keep the app free of code for any one extension, the bundled ones included. The app doesn't read an extension's tables, settings, chat slot, prompt blocks or macros, doesn't name an extension in its prompts, its screens or its routes, and behaves the same whichever extensions are installed. An extension reaches into the app through the doors this guide describes, and the app only ever knows it as "an extension".

One exception: the app declares the bundled RisuAI Realm's provider row itself (its name, its site, its icon and the risu: search operator), so a Realm link in your library keeps its name and risu: keeps filtering while that extension is off. Nothing else in the app names an extension.

The bundled extensions are not part of the app either. They ship as working examples and use the same public doors yours does, which is what makes them proof that those doors are enough.

Why we hold to this, even when a shortcut would be quicker:

  • Extensions come and go. Any of them can be off, removed, a version behind or ahead, or on for one account and off for another. App code that expected one would have to handle all of that, everywhere, for every extension it knew.
  • It keeps your design yours. Once the app reads your tables or your block format, you couldn't change them without breaking it.
  • It scales. A special case for one extension invites the next ten, while a generic door serves every extension, including ones nobody has written yet.
  • Switching off stays honest. Everything an extension adds goes through doors the app tracks, so switching it off takes all of it away. App code that fetched an extension's data itself would leave no trace of what it pulled in, and no way to turn it off.

Your data

Keep your extension's data in places that are yours, and change the app's data through the app's own functions.

  1. Keep your data in your own places. Configuration in ctx.settings, working state in ctx.storage, records in your own x_<your id>_ tables, and on a card or a chat only what should travel with it into an export (card.data.extensions.<your id>, ctx.chatSlot). "Where to store what" has the details.
  2. Write the app's data through ctx.stores rather than your own SQL. A character is not one row. The card you see is put together on every read from the character's row and the rows of its tags, site links and lorebook, so a tag written into the stored card is never seen. A save through the stores also keeps the character's own figures current, such as its token count, and a raw write leaves them stale. It skips the store guards, and every other open tab keeps showing the old data, because nothing tells it. And the app's updates assume its data looks the way the app left it, so a raw write can break an upgrade or be undone by one. Reading the app's tables with SQL works, but the stores give you the finished record.
  3. Keep your state out of the card's own fields. The description, the creator's notes and the tags are what the model reads, what the reader sees and what an export carries to other apps, so your state there changes all three. Put what should travel in card.data.extensions.<your id>, and the rest in your own tables.
  4. Keep one account's data in that account. Use req.db and ctx.files.user(req) for the account that made the request. Avoid holding one account's data in a variable your module keeps between requests, or putting an account's data in ctx.files.shared().
  5. Leave other extensions' data alone (see "Other extensions").

Changing what the app does

An extension doesn't need to change the app's code: it changes what the app does through the doors the app opens for it. Hooks and filters reshape a prompt, a model call or a reply; slots put your interface in named places; commands and events let you act and listen; the stores write the library the way the app itself does. The app keeps these doors stable across releases (a door on its way out is listed under "Deprecated doors" a release ahead), and switching your extension off removes everything you added through them.

Three things look like shortcuts but cost more than they save:

  • Editing the app's own files in your install. That isn't an extension: the next update overwrites it, nobody else can install it, and the license lets you share a changed copy only as a fork on GitHub.
  • Patching the running app: replacing a function on one of the window bridges, rewriting parts of the page the app draws, or reaching into the server's modules from your server half. Nothing stops you (see "Beyond the stable surface"), but it breaks on the next release, switching your extension off doesn't undo it, and two extensions doing it break each other.
  • Asking the app to special-case your extension. "Asking for a new door" describes a request that becomes a door everyone can use.

Other extensions

Please leave another extension's tables, settings, storage and chat slot alone, a bundled one's included. Its author can change them in any release, and it may not be installed, or may be off for this account. If you need what another extension knows, it has to publish it, for example as a command you call (an extension's commands are named <its id>.<name>); otherwise keep the state yourself. The bundled extensions are MIT licensed, so copying one into your own and changing it is fine.

Asking for a new door

When the doors here can't do what you need, ask for one: that's how the API grows. What helps a request become a door:

  • The capability, in general terms. For example "llm.request is told which chat an Assistant call is for", or "a slot on the Assistant's replies, like message.action". Describe it without your extension's name, id, tables or data shape, so it fits any extension.
  • What you are building, in a line or two.
  • What you tried with the doors there are, and where it stopped.
  • A second, unrelated extension that could use it.
  • What it changes with no extensions installed. Ideally nothing: a door does nothing until something walks through it.

File it as an issue. A path into the app's code is welcome as a pointer to where a door might go; the door itself is the design.

How one request took shape. An author wanted the Chat Assistant to see and change what a trackers extension keeps. The first request asked for the app's Assistant to read the bundled Trackers' state and the extensions' prompt blocks, for a badge for active trackers, and for an Assistant reply format for tracker edits. Each of those would have tied the app to one extension, so the work moved to the extension's side, where it fits: keep the state in your own extension (or your own copy of Trackers); add it to the Assistant's material with an assistant source (ctx.assistant.addSource, since API 2.9; before it, an llm.request filter for purpose === 'assistant'); act on the Assistant's answer in a reply.final filter, which on the Assistant is told the chat and the session; and show what the Assistant can see in your own window or settings. The request also found a real gap: llm.request wasn't told which chat an Assistant call was for. That was a door any extension could use, and it shipped in API 2.7.

For your coding assistant

Hand these to your coding assistant as firm rules:

  • Leave the app's own code as it is: don't edit its files, don't patch it while it runs, and don't propose app changes that read, name, special-case or depend on a particular extension, the bundled ones included.
  • Before building anything, look for an existing hook, slot, event, command or store function in extensions.d.ts.
  • If nothing fits, don't work around the app: write a request in the shape of "Asking for a new door", and build the rest inside the extension.
  • Write the app's data only through ctx.stores. Keep your own data in your settings, your storage, your x_<your id>_ tables, or your slot on a card or a chat.
  • Don't read or write another extension's tables, settings, storage or chat slot.
  • When relaying a maintainer's answer, keep what was agreed apart from what was declined: "looking into a generic door" doesn't mean "the app will build our feature".

Installing

Extensions live in extensions/installed/<id>/ on the server, beside the app rather than under data/: a fresh clone, a git clean or a backup of data/ alone does not carry your installs, so back up extensions/installed/ with data/. Three ways in:

  1. Settings > Extensions > Install (admin): paste a plain https:// git repository URL. The server clones it, validates the manifest and files it under its id; the row's update button later runs a fast-forward git pull. The server's git never stops to ask for a sign-in: a repository that wants credentials the server's git does not already hold is refused at once, in git's own words.
  2. Settings > Extensions > Install from a file (admin): a zip of the extension's folder, with manifest.json at the archive's root or inside its one folder (the shape a repository's "Download ZIP" makes), validated and filed like a clone. A .git folder inside is left out, so a zip install has no update button (remove it and install the new zip), and so is the __MACOSX folder macOS adds. The whole archive is refused, naming the reason and writing nothing, when it is not a readable zip, holds a path outside its folder or one a server cannot hold (.., a drive, a device name such as con, a character Windows refuses such as ?, on every server), names a file twice, uses a compression other than stored or deflate, or holds an entry whose bytes disagree with the archive's record of them; and past its limits: 100 MB uploaded, 10,000 entries, 50 MB an entry, 200 MB unpacked. Over the API it is POST /api/extensions/install with an application/zip body.
  3. Copy a folder into extensions/installed/ by hand and press the refresh button in Settings > Extensions. The folder name must equal the manifest id.

A broken install (missing or invalid manifest, id mismatch) shows up in the list with its error instead of vanishing.

Each user then enables the extension for their account with its toggle in Settings > Extensions. Enabling loads it at once and disabling unloads it at once, in every open tab of that account: everything it registered through ctx leaves the page, and its server half stops answering, guarding and listening for that account at the same moment (see "The server half").

Working on an extension

Edit your extension in place, in its folder under extensions/installed/<id>/, and press Reload on its row in Settings > Extensions. The tab runs the code as the folder now holds it: what the extension registered leaves first (its dispose runs), the manifest is read again, and every file of it is fetched fresh, not only the entry, as long as you import your own files with relative paths (./panel.js). A reload that fails says why and leaves nothing registered; fix the file and press Reload again. Reload changes nothing on the server, so any account can use it, and it applies an update (the row's git pull) to the tab without reloading the page.

The server half is not reloaded: Node cannot drop a module it has loaded, so a change to your server code needs the server restarted.

Testing your extension

tools/extension-kit/index.mjs boots Project Σ's own server inside your test's process, on a temporary home, with your extension's folder installed before it starts. Your test then asks your routes what a reader's page would ask, reads the changes your writes reported and pins the plans your tables are read by. The worked example is extensions/bundled/hello-world/test/hello-world.test.mjs; run it with node --test extensions/bundled/hello-world/test/hello-world.test.mjs.

// my-extension/test/notes.test.mjs
import { test, after } from 'node:test';
import assert from 'node:assert/strict';
import { fileURLToPath } from 'node:url';
import { startSigma } from '/path/to/project-sigma/tools/extension-kit/index.mjs'; // your checkout's kit

// the extension this test sits in, installed before the boot
const sigma = await startSigma({ extensions: [fileURLToPath(new URL('..', import.meta.url))] });
after(() => sigma.close());
const me = sigma.session;           // the one account of a server without accounts
const mine = me.ext('my-extension'); // your routes, under /api/ext/my-extension

test('a note is kept and reported', async () => {
    await me.enable('my-extension');  // an extension is on for an account once it switches it on
    const { id } = await me.importCard({ name: 'Nova' });
    const mark = sigma.changes.length;
    assert.equal((await mine(`/notes/${id}`, 'PUT', { text: 'hi' })).status, 200);
    assert.ok(sigma.changes.slice(mark).some(r => r.extension === 'my-extension'));
});
MemberWhat it is
startSigma({ extensions, accounts, prefix, setup, lint })boots the server: extensions are folders installed first, each under its manifest's id (a folder without a readable manifest, or whose id is an extension bundled with the app, is refused before anything starts, as is one the kit's checks stop); accounts ([{ handle, password, admin }]) makes a server with accounts; setup(dataDir) runs on the data folder before the boot; lint: false skips the checks
lintExtension(dir)runs the kit's checks on a folder without booting, answering what they found ([{ file, line, check, name, why }], empty when it passes)
sigma.sessionthe one account of a server without accounts (null when there are accounts)
sigma.signIn(handle, password, { clientId })signs an account in and answers its session; clientId is the tab id its writes carry as their origin (8 to 64 letters, digits or dashes)
session.api(path, method, body)a request under /api, answered as { status, body }
session.ext(id)the same for an extension's routes, under /api/ext/<id>
session.enable(id, on)switches an extension on or off for that account, as its row in Settings > Extensions does
session.importCard(card)imports a card (a V2 or V3 card, or just its data) as a file upload is, answering the import's row
session.apiRequest(path, method, body, opts)the raw request a module's CoreAPI.apiRequest makes, answering the Response
sigma.changesevery committed change record since the boot, each with its account (owner), as the feed delivers them
sigma.db(handle)that account's library, the database your routes read and write
sigma.explain(handle, sql, ...params)the plan SQLite picks for a statement there, line by line
sigma.analyze(handle)statistics on that library, as a running server gathers them
sigma.seed(handle, n)n characters in that library in one write, for a plan pinned at scale; answers their ids
sigma.tick(extId, scheduleId)a ctx.jobs.schedule tick now (the kit arms no timers): its job queued for every account that has the extension on; answers how many
sigma.close()stops the server and takes the temporary home away
  • One server a process: a second startSigma in the same process is refused. node --test runs each test file in a process of its own, so a file is one server.
  • Import the kit first and the app's server modules after startSigma: the server reads its data folder once, when its first module loads. A server module imported earlier read the default folder, your real library, so startSigma refuses to boot and names that folder.
  • The kit checks each extension's own scripts against "Your data" before it boots, and stops the run with the file, the line and the reason for each problem it finds: app-table-write, the app's tables changed with your own SQL (the list read from the app's own schema); other-extension-table, a table named x_ that is not yours; browser-storage, localStorage or sessionStorage. Your tests (a test folder, a .test. or .spec. file) and node_modules are not checked. A line that means it passes with a // sigma-kit-allow: <check> comment on it or the line above. The checks catch honest mistakes, not hidden ones: SQL built from pieces passes them.
  • A plan can move with statistics. Pin the reads your routes and your query doors make at a size that matters, with analyze and without: once SQLite has statistics on a table of a few rows it scans it whatever its indexes, and at scale it reads it by its index.

session.page() opens, in node, the page that account's tab runs, for your client half: the app's own extension host loads it into a small document your code shares (document while the page is open), with the shell's HTML escaping, store verbs, connections read and settings (read from the server, written back a key at a time) and its notifications menu, where a section you register gets its node. The app's own commands stand in and record what they are asked, so an extension that runs or wraps one at init loads. A provider is recorded the same way: ctx.providers.register answers without running your provider's init or refusing a taken id, and its settings section renders into a bare object, so try a provider in the running app. Imports of the app's modules (/modules/...) resolve to the app's files, window is the shell your ctx.CoreAPI is, the document has a documentElement holding its body, and there is no layout: every element measures as an empty box at the origin. A page is a tab: its writes carry a tab id of its own, and one page is open at a time.

test('the chip shows the count', async () => {
    const page = await me.page();
    try {
        await page.boot();                                  // what the account has on, as the page loads it
        const row = (await me.api(`/characters?count=false&ids=${id}`)).body.items[0];
        assert.deepEqual(page.host.badgesFor(row).map(c => c.label), ['1']);
        assert.equal(await page.registry.runCommand('my-extension.greet', { to: 'you' }), 'Hello, you!');
    } finally {
        page.close();
    }
});
MemberWhat it is
page.boot()loads what the account has on, as the page's boot does
page.enable(id, on)the switch on your manager row: the server, then this page
page.openNotifications()opens the notifications menu as a tap on its bell does: each section showing paints into its node through its onOpen
page.registrythe commands, events and hooks your extension joined (runCommand, listCommands, runFilter, ...)
page.hostwhat the app's surfaces ask the host: badgesFor(row), getActions(slot), runAction(key, context), renderDetailTab(key, pane, context), enterView(name, el, { first }), refreshView(name), getChatWindows(), renderSettingsSection(id, el)
page.CoreAPIthe shell your extension is handed; replace a member your test needs
page.logwhat the shell was asked: toasts, opened (chats), overlays, confirms, commands (the app's own, standing in)
page.answerswhat the stand-ins answer: confirm (a confirm dialog) and mobile (whether the page is a phone's)
page.domcreateElement and mountPoint: a small DOM to render a tab, a view, a window or a section into
page.settled()the settings writes still on their way
page.listen()hears the account's changes over the feed, as the page does, so a write made elsewhere reaches your listeners
page.close()unloads what the page loaded and gives the process its document back

Anatomy

my-extension/
  manifest.json
  client/
    index.js        the client entry (ES module)
    style.css       optional stylesheet

manifest.json:

{
    "id": "my-extension",
    "name": "My Extension",
    "version": "1.0.0",
    "author": "you",
    "description": "One sentence about what it does.",
    "requires": { "api": 2 },
    "client": "client/index.js",
    "style": "client/style.css"
}
  • id: lowercase [a-z0-9-], 2 to 60 chars, and it must equal the folder name. Your data on a card lives at card.data.extensions.<id>, so unless the extension is a provider the id may not be a key the app keeps there (a provider's namespace, cl, fav, chat, world) or one other card tools write there for their own data (risuai, talkativeness). Nor may your table prefix (x_<id>_, hyphens as underscores) fall under another installed extension's (a and a-b), nor may the id be jobs, a path the app's own extension routes answer.
  • client: the ES module the host imports. Optional: a server-only extension mounts its routes with no client load at all, and a style-only extension (a theme) ships just a style entry.
  • style: optional; loaded into the module CSS layer, below the user's theme settings and custom CSS in the cascade, so users can restyle your surfaces the way they restyle the app's.
  • requires.api: the extension API version you wrote against: a 'major.minor' string such as '2.1', or a whole number, which means that major's .0 (2 is '2.0'; a range such as "^3" makes the row broken). The major moves when a door breaks, the minor with each batch of doors added within it, so require the minor that brought the newest door you use. An install of an extension that needs a newer api than the app speaks is refused, and one already in place (an update that asks for more) is a broken row; either way the reason names both versions, and neither half loads: the client half is not imported and the server half is not mounted. Current version: '2.9'. What each minor added (api 2 as it stood before minors were counted is '2.0': every door on this page not listed here):
    • '2.1': ctx.prompt.fence (with its char option) and profileSetting on a ctx.llm call (server); the version itself as 'major.minor' (ctx.apiVersion on both halves, a string), and requires.api taking one.
    • '2.2': the chat.card.action slot (addAction('chatCard', spec)), a verb in a chat's menu in the chats view.
    • '2.3': ctx.files.user(req) and ctx.files.shared() (server), your two folders as handles that keep every name inside them and write each file whole.
    • '2.4': the composer.action slot (addAction('composer', spec)), a button in a strip above the conversation's composer; the chat.compose and chat.send commands, text into the composer and sent as the reader; ctx.server.stream, an answer from your server half read a line at a time, and a signal on ctx.server.call; when on a chat window (addChatWindow), which decides whether the window has its doors (its header button and its phone tile), and close on the context a window's hooks are handed, which closes it as its own close button does; CoreAPI.removeNotificationSection(id), which takes a notifications section out, and a section you add through registerNotificationSection leaving when you are switched off.
    • '2.5': CoreAPI.whenLibraryTotal(), the library's count once the server has counted it (a promise; null when the count could not be read), since CoreAPI.getLibraryTotal() answers null until then rather than a guess; and CoreAPI.openUserGuide(section), the user guide in a tab of its own, at a page ('online') or one of its headings ('online/janitorai'); CoreAPI.getPersonaPronouns(), the active persona's pronoun forms ({ sub, obj, poss, possP, ref }, null when none is read or there is no persona), and CoreAPI.answerPronounMacros(text, forms), the five pronoun macros in plain text answered from a persona's forms as the prompt answers them (null forms leave them raw); CoreAPI.openConversationForCharacter(char), a character's latest chat opened, or their first one started (the details' Chat button; resolves whether one opened); ctx.keys.register(spec), a keyboard shortcut of your own in the app's keymap, rebound by the user in Settings > Keyboard; CoreAPI.randomId(length), a random id of lower-case letters and digits (12 long by default); CoreAPI.formatRelativeAge(when), how long ago a time was in the app's short form ('just now', '5m ago', '2d ago'); CoreAPI.galleryKindOf(item), a media item's or file name's kind ('image', 'video', 'audio', or null), the server's kind first; CoreAPI.renderEditableTagChips(container, tags, onRemove), tag chips with a remove button each, in the app's look; CoreAPI.wireDragReorder(list, { item, handle, onReorder }), drag to reorder a list's items (a touch drags from the handle), wired once per list; CoreAPI.clampTagRows(container, look), a list of tag pills cut to the rows that fit (three, two on a phone) with a +N pill that flips to Show less, kept fitted through resizes, the look naming your classes ({ tag, more, overflow }, and optionally collapsed and expanded for the list itself); CoreAPI.tagStateLook(state), the icon class and title the app's three-way tag toggles wear for 'include', 'exclude' and anything else (neutral); and chatId on a ctx.llm call (server), the chat it is made for, whose connection answers it when you name none.
    • '2.6': pictures on a ctx.llm call (server): images on a user's message, as bytes or a reference to the account's own portrait, gallery image or persona picture (see "Calling a model"); ctx.llm.canSee(db, request), whether the connection that call would use sends pictures; and capabilities.vision on a kind of your own, which hands your adapter a user turn's pictures (see "A kind of connection of your own"). A chat's own gallery: ctx.stores.attachments (server), its pictures and which messages show them, and the attachment change it reports (created, deleted, carrying the chat's id), which the feed carries and a store guard can refuse (store.before.attachment.created, store.before.attachment.deleted). A ChatMessage answers its pictures as attachments. CoreAPI.liveFormattingOn(surface), whether the reader's Format as it streams applies to 'chat', 'assistant' or 'creator', so a panel painting a reply as it arrives can pass streaming: 'live' to CoreAPI.formatChatText (with openIslands: false where markup is talked about rather than drawn, so an unclosed html tag stays text until it closes); CoreAPI.aiConnectionStreams(surface), whether the connection block mounted for that surface has Stream the reply on; and CoreAPI.lockMinWidth(el, measure), which holds a control at the widest of the states measure() lays out (it returns their widest scrollWidth), so a label or count that changes never shifts what sits beside it; a control not shown yet locks when it first shows.
    • '2.7': ctx.browser.consumer (server), a hosted-browser consumer of your own for a Cloudflare-gated site, and ctx.browser.fetch (page), a fetch inside the hosted browser through it; an inventory row lists a half's consumers as browserConsumers; and llm.request is told which chat or session its call is for (chatId, sessionId, charId, mode, as reply.final names them).
