    • '2.9': ctx.assistant.addSource (server), a context source the chat assistant can be given, with its own switch in the assistant's context view; and ctx.CoreAPI.confirmDiscardEdits(subject) (client), the app's own "Discard unsaved edits?" ask, Keep Editing beside Discard, resolving true when the reader lets the typing go.
    • '2.8': regex scripts (server): ctx.stores.regex, the reader's own scripts, each preset's, a character's (on its card, as SillyTavern keeps them) and whether a preset's or a character's list may run, and the two changes it reports, regexScript (created, updated, deleted) and regexConsent (updated), which the feed carries and a store guard can refuse (store.before.regexScript.created, store.before.regexScript.updated, store.before.regexScript.deleted, store.before.regexConsent.updated). hideForeign: true on CoreAPI.formatChatText draws text as a chat message is drawn: comments and tags that are not HTML hidden, their text kept (left out, a reply that talks about markup keeps every tag it names). CoreAPI.showTagAutocomplete(text, { dropdown, exclude, onPick, allowCreate, limit }) fills the .tag-autocomplete element dropdown with the library's tags that contain the text (up to limit, 10 by default), leaving out the names in exclude in any case (none by default), and shows it; a click on one hands its name to onPick, and text nothing matches offers a Create row unless allowCreate is false. dropdown and onPick are required: without them nothing is asked or shown. An answer for text typed over since is dropped. The list opens above its positioned parent, or under it with the is-below class. Call CoreAPI.tagAutocompleteKeydown(event, dropdown) first in the field's keydown: the arrow keys light a suggestion and Enter picks it, and it answers true when it took the key. CoreAPI.uiZoom() answers the factor the reader's UI Scale zooms the page by (1 at 100% and on a phone): divide a rect read with getBoundingClientRect by it to place something in the page's css pixels, such as a popover at a button. CoreAPI.numberSetting(key) answers a numeric setting as the reader set it, or its shipped default when the stored value is no number (0 is an answer, never replaced). CoreAPI.diffSplitView(before, after, { heads }) answers the app's side-by-side diff of two texts as HTML (html, empty when they are the same) with the lines changed, added and removed: changed lines in pairs, each changed word marked, unchanged lines away from a change folded, the two column heads yours to name (Now and Proposed by default). CoreAPI.queueMediaDownloads(target) puts characters on the background media queue, where each one's remote media is scanned and downloaded: target is a selection (CoreAPI.getSelection()), { ids } or { all: true } (the whole library, the ones already processed left out). A character named in a selection is scanned again even if it was done before. It answers the server's counts (queued, noMedia for the ones with no remote media and no provider link, active for the ones already downloading) and says them in a toast. CoreAPI.downloadMediaForCharacter(char) queues one character's media on the background queue, as a card's right-click menu does (the details gallery's button shows its own dialog).

An extension written against api 1 loads unchanged (api 2 only added doors) unless it meets one of the refusals named on this page: an id past 60 characters or equal to a reserved key, a table prefix under another extension's, or a provider whose id is not its namespace (see "Provider extensions").

  • settings: optional object of default values for your per-user config keys (ctx.settings / ctx.setting). Declare a default for every key you read.
  • contributes: optional array naming what your extension adds, so the manager can say so before it is enabled: chat.window, chat.action, message.action, character.action, app.action, character.card.badge, character.details.tab, character.bulk.action, shell.view, lore.entry.action, chat.card.action, composer.action. Anything else makes the install a broken row. When you declare the list, the host warns in the console if your code uses a slot it does not name.
  • lazy: optional; your menu verbs, windows and views declared here load your code at their first use instead of at boot (see "Loading on first use").

Only the folders holding your client and style entries are served (at /ext/<id>/...), and an entry can import siblings from inside its own folder. Put server code in a folder of its own (say server/): that folder is never served. manifest.json and package.json at the root are never served either, nor is the scratch directory. If your client entry sits at the root, the whole root is served except those two files and your server entry, so keep anything private in a folder of its own.

Loading on first use

An extension whose way in is a menu verb, a window or a view can leave its code unloaded until someone uses one. Declare those entry points in the manifest:

"contributes": ["app.action", "chat.window", "shell.view"],
"lazy": {
    "actions": [{ "slot": "app", "id": "open", "label": "Open my panel", "icon": "fa-bolt" }],
    "windows": [{ "id": "notes", "label": "Notes", "icon": "fa-note-sticky", "size": { "w": 40, "h": 60 } }],
    "views": [{ "id": "board", "label": "Board", "icon": "fa-table" }]
}

Switched on, the extension's verbs, windows and views appear from these lines alone, and neither its code nor its stylesheet is fetched. The first run of a verb, open of a window or entry to a view loads the code once (however many uses arrive together) and runs your init; your own ctx.ui.addAction, addChatWindow and ui.contribute('shell.view', ...) of the same ids then take the declared places, and that first use goes to your code with the context it was given. So:

  • Register every declared entry point in init, with the same slot and id. One you declare but never register is taken away once your code has loaded, with a warning in the console.
  • A declaration holds only the static parts: an action's slot, id, label and icon; a window's id, label, icon and, optionally, its first size and min; a view's id, label and icon. Each follows the registrars' rules (an id of lowercase letters, digits and dashes starting with a letter, at most 32; a label of at most 40 characters, 24 for a view; a Font Awesome solid icon), each kind is one your contributes names when you declare it, and there is at least one. Anything else makes the install a broken row.
  • An action's when() is code, so a declared verb shows wherever its slot shows until the code runs; if its first use is one your when() refuses, the reader is told it does not apply there. A window's when() is code too: a declared window keeps its doors until the code runs, and once it has, a when() that says no takes them away and closes the window.
  • Hooks, event listeners, badges, tabs and a settings section register only once your code runs. An extension that needs any of them from the start stays eager: leave lazy out.
  • The manager shows a lazy extension switched on as "on first use" until its code has run. Reload (see "Working on an extension") puts the declared entry points back and fetches the code again at their next use.

The bundled Library Stats loads this way.

The client entry

Export a default object with init(ctx). It runs when your extension loads: at startup, after the app's startup modules have initialized, or the moment a user switches it on. A few feature modules load on first use instead (the Chats view, batch tagging, the tags manager, batch transfer): reach them through commands, CoreAPI and events, never by assuming they are already on the page.

export default {
    async init(ctx) {
        ctx.log('ready');
    },
    // optional: runs when the user switches the extension off
    dispose() {},
};

Switching off. When a user switches your extension off, the host first undoes everything you registered through ctx, newest first: commands and wrappers, event listeners, hooks, actions, windows (an open one closes, and its onClose runs), views, tabs, badges, overlays, settings and provider sections, a registered provider, what you registered through ctx.CoreAPI (onViewEnter, onViewExit, onCharactersDeleted, storeWatch, onJob, and a notifications section from registerNotificationSection), and your stylesheet. Then it calls your dispose(). Undo there only what you did outside ctx: a node you added to the page, a timer, a listener on window. A dispose() that answers a promise is waited for before your extension loads again (switched back on, or a Reload), for up to five seconds; one that has not settled by then is logged and the load goes on. Your dispose() runs whenever your init ran, so an init that throws gets it too, once what it registered has been undone, and so does one the user switched off while it was still running, once it settles. The browser keeps your module loaded, so switching the extension back on runs init again on the same module: keep your state inside init, or reset it in dispose.

ctx is the api 2 surface, the part we promise not to break casually: a door leaves only after "Deprecated doors" has listed it.

FieldWhat it is
ctx.manifestyour row in the server's inventory (ManifestRow in the published types): your manifest.json as the server read it, keys it does not know dropped, plus bundled, git and enabled, and with a server half its state (serverState, serverError), what it registered (serverHooks), what its hooks cost this account (serverCalls) and its query doors (serverQuery)
ctx.apiVersionthe host's extension api version, 'major.minor' (see requires.api under "Anatomy")
ctx.CoreAPIthe CoreAPI the app's own modules use, every function it exports: settings, toasts, characters, search, modals, lorebooks, media, the HTTP API. This is the main event. A function's name stays until "Deprecated doors" lists it; what it takes and answers is the app's own and is not typed (see "Beyond the stable surface"). What you register through onViewEnter, onViewExit, onCharactersDeleted, storeWatch or onJob answers its removal and is undone when your extension is switched off. A section you add to the notifications menu with registerNotificationSection({ id, getStatus, onOpen }) answers its node and is taken out when you are switched off, or sooner with removeNotificationSection(id). The id is yours alone: the app holds it under your extension's name (the node's data-notif-section reads ext:<your-id>/<id>), so it never replaces one of the app's sections or another extension's, and removeNotificationSection(id) takes only your own. Anything another CoreAPI call adds to the page is your dispose()'s to take away
ctx.log(...)console.log prefixed with your id
ctx.commands.run(id, args) / list()run a command by id and get its answer: the app's own verbs and every extension's (see "Commands"). list() names them all, with their titles, who wraps them and, for the app's own, the types of their args and answer
ctx.commands.register(name, fn, { title })add a command of your own, registered as <your-id>.<name> so it can never take the app's id or another extension's; fn gets one args object and its return value is the answer
ctx.commands.wrap(id, wrapper, { priority, override })wrap any command: wrapper(next, args) can hand on, change the args or the answer, or answer on its own. Returns the unwrap
ctx.keys.register({ id, label, key, scope, run })a keyboard shortcut of your own, <your-id>.<id>: run() is called on key while scope is in front ('global', the default, 'library' or 'conversation'). Settings > Keyboard lists it under your extension's name, where the user can rebind or unbind it. A key is written as the app writes it ('Mod+Shift+H', 'H', '/'; Mod is Cmd on a Mac and Ctrl elsewhere); one with no Mod, Ctrl or Alt never fires while the user types in a field. A key the app's own shortcuts or another extension's already use there is refused with the clash named; leave key out to ship the action unbound. It leaves with your extension. Returns the full id
ctx.events.on / off(name, fn)listen to the app's events. A name ctx.events.list() names hands fn its payload, an object of ids; any other name is a plain DOM event listener handed the Event, as in api 1 (see "Events")
ctx.events.list()the listed events, their payload keys and each key's type
ctx.events.emit(name, detail)dispatch an event namespaced as ext:<your-id>:<name>
ctx.hooks.add(name, fn, { priority }) / list()the conversation's hooks (list names them with their kind and types): a filter answers the value to hand on and an action is told, highest priority first; see "Conversation hooks"
ctx.server.call(path, method, body, { signal }) / stream(path, method, body, { signal })a request to your own server half: path is under /api/ext/<your-id>, method is GET and body none unless you name them. call answers the parsed body; a refused request throws an Error in the server's own words (its error, else HTTP <status>), carrying status and the parsed body as data. stream (a POST unless you name the method) is the same request read as it arrives, an async iterable: each line of an application/x-ndjson answer, parsed, as it lands, or the parsed body once for any other answer; a refusal throws as call's does (see "Streaming from your server half"). A signal stops either, the request aborting with an AbortError and your route hearing the hang-up
ctx.messages.shown(msg) / digest(text)the text a message shows (its active alternate when it has several, else mes) and a digest of a text, eight hex digits. Your server half's ctx.messages answers alike, so a digest your server stored beside what it made of a message tells the page whether the message still shows those words
ctx.storage.load() / save(doc)a per-user JSON document stored server-side under your id. Stateless on purpose: every load fetches and every save writes, and failures throw instead of quietly becoming an empty document. Cache it yourself if you need to, and drop the copy when ctx.CoreAPI.storeWatch('ext-<your id>', fn) calls fn: another tab, device or process wrote it
ctx.settings.get(key) / set(key, value) / defaultsper-user CONFIG with defaults you declare in manifest.json under "settings". Keys are scoped to your extension on the wire (ext:<id>:<key>), so you can never collide with the app's keys or another extension's. get returns the stored value, or your declared default when nothing is stored or the stored value is null or '' (a stored false or 0 wins). set takes effect in memory at once and is saved within about half a second; a failed save shows a toast and the value goes back to what the server holds
ctx.chats.open(chatId, { messageId, seq })open a chat by its id, from any view, and bring a message into view (scrolled to and briefly marked): messageId names it wherever it sits now, seq names a place, and messageId wins (one gone since reveals nothing). Always answers a promise, true when the chat is the open conversation, false for an unknown id or a failed read. A chat that is already the conversation is resumed, never reloaded. See "Opening a chat"
ctx.chats.follow(spec)one chat's data read while you show it, for a window that follows the conversation: read(chatId) reads it and apply(data, chatId) paints it (null when no chat is shown); the newest read wins and a read that lands after its chat left the screen is dropped. Answers { show(chatId), reload(), reloadSoon(), rest(), isOpen(), chatId() }; reloadSoon waits soonMs (400) for the writes to settle, working(data) true reads again every pollMs (2000) while shown, fail(error, chatId) hears a failed read and moved(chatId, was) the shown chat changing, before its read
ctx.ui.contribute(slotId, spec)fill a slot, one of the places the app opens to extensions; ctx.ui.slots() lists them. The four add... calls below are shorthand for it: contribute('chat.action', spec) is addAction('chat', spec), contribute('chat.window', spec) is addChatWindow(spec), contribute('extension.settings', { render }) is addSettingsSection(render) and contribute('provider.settings', { providerId, render, excludeTagsHint }) is addProviderSettingsSection. It throws on an unknown slot or a spec missing what the slot needs
ctx.ui.slots()every slot with its kind, what its context carries and each key's type, the spec it takes, and how it reaches a phone
ctx.ui.addSettingsSection(render)contribute a settings body; it renders inside your extension's row in Settings > Extensions every time the manager draws its list (opening the panel, a refresh, a toggle), so keep render repeatable. It throws when render is not a function
ctx.providers.register(provider, manifestRow)for provider extensions: register a character source, its ProviderBase instance and its provider row; see "Provider extensions"
ctx.browser.fetch(name, path, opts)a same-origin fetch inside the hosted browser, through the consumer your server half registered under name (ctx.browser.consumer there): path stays under its prefix, opts takes headers (up to 8), method (GET or POST), jsonBody, timeoutMs and signal. Answers { status, body, retryAfter }; the browser is the one the account set up under Settings > Online > Hosted Browser
ctx.ui.addProviderSettingsSection(providerId, render, opts)for provider extensions: the body of a provider section in Settings > Online. The host builds the chrome (name, Beta badge and position from your provider row) and appends the standard exclude-tags group, which your row gets by default (excludeTagKey defaults to your provider id; null for none, and opts.excludeTagsHint overrides its hint line). Call it after ctx.providers.register; the section exists only while your extension is loaded. It throws without the provider id or a render function
ctx.ui.addChatWindow(spec)a window inside a conversation: the host builds the window (the chrome, the header button, the phone's tile) and you render the body; see "A window in the conversation"
ctx.ui.addOverlay(spec)put an overlay you build yourself in the app's overlay stack, so Escape and a phone's back close it; answers the element id to give it. See "An overlay of your own"
ctx.ui.dialog(spec)a dialog in the app's own shell, on top of addOverlay: the host builds its chrome and closes it, you fill its body. See "A dialog of your own"
ctx.ui.connectionSelect(select, { key })the account's connections appended to a <select> of yours (its own first option stands for the one the chats use), the value stored under key (profileId by default) chosen, each pick kept under it, and the select given the app's look; key: null keeps nothing (a pick that rides one request). Answers { gone } once the options are in: gone is true when the stored pick names a connection the account does not have (the select shows the default, which is what runs, and the pick stays stored until changed), never when the read failed
ctx.ui.addAction(slot, spec)a verb in one of the app's menus: chat (the conversation's options menu), message (a message row's overflow), character (the character context menu), app (the topbar menu), bulk (a multi-selection's context menu and the multi-select toolbar), lore (an open lorebook entry, in the lorebook manager and a chat's Lorebooks window), chatCard (a chat's menu in the chats view), composer (a button in the strip above the conversation's composer). The host renders it on desktop and on the phone; see "Actions in the menus"

Types for your editor

docs/extensions.d.ts types everything on this page. On the client: ctx and each of its calls, every command's args and answer, every event's payload, every conversation hook's value, context and answer, and every slot's context and the spec it takes. On the server: its ctx, every pipeline's value and facts, every store guard's name and the change record. Copy it beside your entries (or point at it in a checkout of the app) and your editor completes and checks your code, JavaScript included:

/** @type {import('./extensions').ClientExtension} */
export default {
    async init(ctx) {
        ctx.events.on('character.updated', ({ ids, fields }) => {
            // ids and fields are readonly string[]; a key the payload lacks is an error
        });
        const done = await ctx.commands.run('tags.apply', { ids: ['<a character id>'], add: ['favorite'] });
        // done is { affected, skipped, deltas }; a refused change throws
    },
};
/** @type {import('./extensions').ServerExtension['register']} */
export function register(router, ctx) {
    ctx.hooks.add('reply.final', (text, { surface }) => (surface === 'chat' ? text.trim() : undefined));
    // a filter answering anything but the pipeline's value is an error
}

Every command, event, hook, slot, pipeline and store guard the app opens is in it, with the shape the app hands, and ctx.commands.list(), ctx.events.list(), ctx.hooks.list() and ctx.ui.slots() carry the client's types at run time, as TypeScript text. Not in it: an extension's own commands (their args and answers are that extension's), the signatures of ctx.CoreAPI's functions (their names are kept, as "Deprecated doors" says; what each takes and answers follows the app's releases), and the functions of the server's ctx.stores modules, which follow the server's releases.

The types file, the code examples in this guide and the test kit (tools/extension-kit/) are licensed under the BSD Zero Clause License (0BSD) as well as the app's own license: copy them into your extension and publish it on any terms. Every bundled extension (extensions/bundled/), Hello World among them, is under the MIT License: copy one as the start of your own and publish it on any terms, keeping its copyright notice and its LICENSE file with it. The LICENSE file says so in full.

Where to store what

Keep what your extension holds in the account's database unless it does not fit there. The database stores are the default because each write is all or nothing, the account's backups copy them, every write reaches the account's open tabs on the change feed, and what rides a card or a chat travels with it into its export:

  • ctx.settings - per-user CONFIG: toggles, thresholds, small preferences your settings section renders. Declare every key's default in manifest.json ("settings": { "greeting": "Hello" }); a key you read without declaring has no default and comes back undefined. Keys are scoped to your extension, and both your client and server halves read the declaration and apply the same fallback (nothing stored, null or '' reads your default), so the two halves answer alike.
  • ctx.storage - per-user STATE: documents, queues, working data, anything with structure. One JSON document, loaded and saved whole.
  • Your own tables (server half) - data a character, chat or message owns, and anything you filter or sort by; see "Your own tables". Their writes reach the change feed through ctx.report, and an export through the card.export and chat.export filters.
  • The card or the chat itself - what should travel with them into an export and a branch: card.data.extensions.<your id>, a message's extra.<your id>, a chat's slot (ctx.chatSlot).

Files are for what does not fit there: large blobs, caches you can rebuild, binaries, a file an engine of your own opens (a search index, a SQLite file of yours). The server half keeps them through ctx.files (see "The server half"):

  • ctx.files.user(req) - a folder of yours inside the requesting account's home, for that account's files.
  • ctx.files.shared() - one folder of yours that every account shares, such as a cache, at extensions/installed/.data/<id>/ for bundled and installed extensions alike (CL_EXTENSIONS_DIR moves it with the installs): never served and never inside an install.

An account's backups copy its library database alone, so no file of yours is in them, and no export carries one: keep in files what you can rebuild or can afford to lose, and tell your users when you keep more. Removing your extension deletes its shared folder and its folder in every account's home. ctx.dataDir() and ctx.userDataDir(req) answer the same two folders as plain paths, for code that walks them itself.

Never localStorage (it is per-browser, shared across accounts, and invisible to the server), never the app's own setting keys, never another extension's scope. Credentials go in a settings key of your own, never browser storage.

A window in the conversation

An extension that works on the open chat gets a real window beside the first-party ones, with the same chrome, the same drag and resize, the same place on a phone. Register it once at init; the host does the rest.

ctx.ui.addChatWindow({
    id: 'timeline',                 // [a-z][a-z0-9-]{0,31}, unique within your extension
    label: 'Timeline',              // the window title and the button's tooltip (up to 40 chars)
    icon: 'fa-timeline',            // a Font Awesome solid name
    min: { w: 24, h: 30 },          // optional, percent of the conversation area
    size: { w: 30, h: 60 },         // optional, the first size, percent too (at least min)
    render(bodyEl, { chatId, character, panel, body, close }) {
        // runs ONCE, on first open; render into bodyEl and keep your own state
    },
    onOpen({ chatId, character, close }) { /* every open, after render */ },
    onClose() { /* every close */ },
    follow(detail, { chatId, character }) {
        // a character this tab holds was written: detail.charId names WHICH one, never the
        // character itself, so re-read it: ctx.CoreAPI.getCharacterById(detail.charId)
    },
    // optional: whether the window has its doors now; false hides them and closes it
    when: ({ chatId, character }) => ctx.settings.get('asWindow') === true,
});

when decides whether the window is there at all: while it says no, the window has no header button, no phone tile and no row in Chat settings, and a window that is open when it turns to no is closed (your onClose runs). The window is kept, so its body is as you left it when when says yes again. It is asked, with the conversation's { chatId, character }, every time the doors are laid out: when a window is added or removed, when a conversation opens, its character arrives or it closes, when an extension's setting is written (through ctx.settings.set or in another tab) and when a phone opens its chat options. It must decide synchronously: one that throws or returns a promise hides the window. A window with no when always has its doors. The reader's own choices apply on top of it: where its button sits in the desktop header and whether they hid it there, and the Phone switch in Chat settings.

close, on the context every hook is handed, closes the window the way its own close button does: onClose runs, its opener goes dark, and a phone's back button behaves as after any close. Calling it on a window already closed does nothing. A window that hands its result to the conversation can close itself so the reader sees it land, which matters on a phone, where the window covers the conversation; on a desktop it stands beside it.

follow hears a write to a character this tab holds, once your window has rendered: every write this tab makes (a save anywhere in it, an avatar swap, an assistant edit), and a write made elsewhere (another tab or device) to a character this tab has read. It does not hear a write elsewhere to a character this tab never read, and a write to many characters at once while the tab holds more than a thousand of them reaches it only for the conversation's character and the one whose details are open. Check detail.charId against the character you are showing; detail.source is the app's own name for the surface that wrote and may change in any release. To hear every write to any character, listen to character.updated (see "Events").

  • It opens from a button of its own in the conversation header on desktop (a header too narrow for every button lists the rest under its More button) and from a tile of its own under Windows in the phone's chat options. The reader hides the header button by right-clicking it or in Chat settings, under Conversation header, and the phone tile with its Phone switch in Chat settings, under Extensions. It is the standard window, full screen on a phone.
  • On desktop it first opens at your size (38 by 70 percent when you give none); its size and position then persist per user in your own settings scope (win-<id>-w and friends), and Reset window layouts puts it back at that first size like every other window.
  • A render or hook that throws, or returns a promise that rejects, shows an error line in your window and logs to the console; the conversation keeps working. A bad spec throws at registration with the reason, so a mistyped icon never becomes "my window does not appear".

A view of your own

A whole view beside Characters, Chats and Online, for a surface that is a place of its own rather than a window over a chat:

ctx.ui.contribute('shell.view', {
    id: 'stats',                    // [a-z][a-z0-9-]{0,31}, unique within your extension
    label: 'Stats',                 // up to 24 characters
    icon: 'fa-chart-simple',        // a Font Awesome solid name
    render(el) {
        // the first time the view is entered: build into el and keep your own state
    },
    enter(el) { /* every time the view is entered, the first included, after render has settled */ },
    leave() { /* every time the reader leaves it */ },
    refresh() { /* a phone's pull-to-refresh: re-read what the view shows; leave it out and the pull never arms here */ },
});
  • Its name is ext:<your id>/<view id> (ext:my-extension/stats above): view.switch takes it, ctx.CoreAPI.getCurrentView() answers it while your view is on screen, and an app action's when sees it as its view.
  • It gets a button after Online in the view switcher, and on a phone a row in the More sheet's Views group. It has the whole content area (the app's search and filters step aside while it is on screen), and the phone's swipe between views stays among the app's three.
  • Do the work of a visible view in enter and stop it in leave (a timer, a listener that repaints), so a view nobody is looking at costs nothing.
  • A render may return a promise: the first enter waits for it to settle, so it meets what render built, and does not run at all if the reader left the view before then.
  • Switching your extension off while your view is on screen takes the reader back to Characters first (your leave runs). A render, enter or leave that throws, or returns a promise that rejects, shows a toast naming your extension and the view and logs to the console. Registering with a bad id, a missing or long label, a missing or bad icon, an enter or leave that is not a function, or a duplicate id throws with the reason.

An overlay of your own

A surface that is neither a window in the conversation nor a dialog (a full-screen view of your own, a panel of a shape of your own; a dialog is ctx.ui.dialog, below) you build and append yourself; register it once at init so the app closes it like its own: Escape on the desktop, and a phone's back button, topmost first.

let dialog = null;
const close = () => { dialog?.remove(); dialog = null; };
const overlayId = ctx.ui.addOverlay({ id: 'dialog', close });   // answers 'ext-<your id>-dialog'

function open() {
    dialog = document.createElement('div');
    dialog.id = overlayId;          // the stack finds your overlay by this id
    document.body.appendChild(dialog);
}
  • close() is how the stack closes it; it runs on Escape and on a phone's back, so it must take the overlay off the page. visible(el) is optional; by default an overlay counts as open while its element is on the page and has no hidden class.
  • It sits above a conversation's windows on the phone's back stack and below a confirm raised over it, so back closes your overlay before the chat under it.
  • Add no Escape listener of your own: the stack already answers it, and a second one closes two things at once.
  • Switching your extension off closes an open overlay: your close runs with { leaving: true }, which means take it off the page without asking, and the host removes the element afterwards whatever your close did. A bad id ([a-z][a-z0-9-]{0,31}), a missing close or an id you already registered throws at registration.

A dialog of your own

Most overlays are dialogs, and the app has one shell for them: ctx.ui.dialog builds it, registers it in the overlay stack and closes it, and you fill its body.

const dialog = ctx.ui.dialog({
    id: 'ask',                      // [a-z][a-z0-9-]{0,31}; the element is ext-<your id>-ask
    label: 'Ask your model',        // its name for a screen reader, and its title; up to 60 characters
    icon: 'fa-comment-dots',        // optional
    className: 'my-dialog',         // optional, on the dialog's box
    size: false,                    // true: resizable from its edges, its size kept (min: { w, h } its floor, in pixels)
    canClose: () => !unsaved || ctx.CoreAPI.confirmDiscardEdits('the question you are typing'),
    onClose: () => { /* stop what the dialog started */ },
});

function open() {
    const body = dialog.open({ title: 'Ask your model' });   // the title may differ per open
    body.innerHTML = '...';
}
  • open() builds the dialog afresh (an open one closes first, unasked), shows it on the next frame and answers its body. close() closes it and answers whether it did; isOpen() and el say whether it is on the page.
  • Escape, a phone's back, a click on the backdrop and its close control dismiss it. canClose(how) is asked first: false, or a promise of false, keeps it open. close({ force: true }) asks nothing, and neither does your extension being switched off, which closes it at once (how.leaving). onClose(how) hears it close by any road.
  • It registers as an overlay, so it throws as addOverlay does on a bad or taken id, and on a missing or long label, a bad icon or a canClose or onClose that is not a function.

Opening a chat

An extension that knows a chat's id (from GET /chats?charId=, a hit of GET /chats/search, or a chat it just created) opens it with one call, here at a search hit's message:

const opened = await ctx.chats.open(hit.chatId, { messageId: hit.messageId });   // the message is optional
if (!opened) ctx.CoreAPI.showToast('That chat could not be opened', 'warning');

This works whatever the Chats list is showing (another page, a filter, a sort that puts the chat far down). messageId is a message's own id (the id of each message GET /chats/{id} answers, a search hit's messageId), which finds it wherever it sits now; seq is its place, which a delete earlier in the chat renumbers, so name a message by its id whenever you have one. The whole conversation is on screen once it opens, so any message can be revealed. The chat.open command is the same verb.

Messages on the wire

GET /chats/{id} answers each message as { id, seq, createdAt, role, name, content, attachments, raw } (ChatMessage in the published types): its own id, never reused and kept when an earlier message's delete moves it down a place; its place, 0 to n - 1; when it was written (unix ms, read from the stored send_date in any SillyTavern spelling, null when it has none); who said it (user, assistant or system); the stored name; its text (the active alternate); the pictures it shows from its chat's gallery, in order; and raw, the stored SillyTavern row, verbatim, where swipes, extra and the rest live (with extra.attachments naming its pictures' ids when it shows any). The same shape answers a send, a message's PATCH, a branch, a chat's create and a generate's done frame; ctx.stores.chats.projectMessages(db, rows) builds it from rows listMessageRows answered, their pictures read in one query (projectMessage({ id, seq, msg }), one row with no database, answers no pictures).

A write addressed by a message's place (PATCH or DELETE /chats/{id}/messages/{seq}, a branch, a generate's targetSeq) names the message it means: ?messageId= on the PATCH and the DELETE, messageId in a branch's body, targetId beside a generate's targetSeq. When another message holds that place by now (a delete elsewhere renumbered the chat since you read it), the write is refused with 409 and nothing is written; read the chat again. Without the id the write goes to whichever message holds the place. A write sends stored-row fields (mes, swipes, extra, ...), never a ChatMessage: seq and raw are refused on a row.

A write that rewrites or grows the message's active alternate can name that alternate as you read it: shown beside the fields of an edit's PATCH (never stored), targetShown beside a regenerate's, a continue's or a swipe's targetId, each { index, count, text } (the active alternate's place, swipes.length or 1 for a message with none, and its text). When the stored message holds another (an alternate added, dropped, picked or rewritten since you read it), the request is refused with 409 before anything is written or the model is asked. Left out, the write acts on whatever alternate is active.

Commands

A command is a verb by id: one args object in, an answer out. The app's own verbs are commands, an extension's verbs join the same list, and any command can be run or wrapped by any extension. Every character verb in the table, and opening the lorebook manager, goes through its command whichever way the reader asks (a button, a menu, a key, a phone's sheet or swipe), so a wrapper hears every path; the other rows say what runs them.

CommandArgsAnswers
chat.open{ chatId, messageId, seq }true when the chat is the open conversation; ctx.chats.open runs it, so a wrapper hears those opens
chat.compose{ text, mode }true once text is in the open conversation's composer as typing would leave it: the box grows to fit and the text is kept as the chat's draft. mode is 'replace' (the default) or 'append', which puts it on a new line after what the box holds. false when no conversation is open, or while the box is held by a send waiting on a composer.send filter. The reader's own typing does not run it. A text that is not a string, or another mode, throws
chat.send{ text }sends text as the reader the way Send does: it replaces what the box holds, the composer.send filters run, the message is stored and a reply is generated. Answers once that reply has run (or failed): true when the message was stored, false when no conversation is open, a reply is on its way, text is blank, a filter took the text, or the message could not be stored (the box keeps it, as it does for Send). The Send button does not run this command: to see every message the reader sends, add a composer.send filter. A text that is not a string throws
character.open{ id, elevated, navList }true when the character was found and its details opened; elevated opens them above a modal already open instead of replacing it. Stepping from one character to the next in the details and the app's own modules run it too; when the app runs it, char also carries the app's object for that character
character.delete{ ids } or { filter, expect }By ids: { deleted, missing }. By filter (a GET /api/characters query string, a Select All's CoreAPI.getSelection().filter): the server fixes the set and deletes it, answering { changed, missing } once done (stopped: true when it was stopped partway); expect is the count the reader confirmed, and a set that holds another count now (another tab imported meanwhile) is refused before anything is deleted, the error carrying total. Either way the delete goes in batches, and a store guard that refuses one stops the run there: nothing of that batch is deleted, the batches before it stay deleted and leave the page, and the command throws with the guard's words (or the reason the write did not land) and how many were deleted before it. A re-import that replaces a character runs it too; it asks for no confirmation of its own
character.favorite{ ids, value } or { filter, value }value is true or false, and the server writes only the characters whose favorite moves. By ids: { affected, skipped }, the ids that moved and the ids no character holds (an id in neither already was). By filter: { changed }, how many moved, and the library is re-read once it lands. A write a store guard refuses throws with the guard's words (or the reason it did not land)
character.update{ id, fields, rootFields, source }{ ok }, with error in the server's words when it refused a field, and told when the reader has already been shown the refusal (the app toasts it once, so a caller adds no toast of its own; a wrapper that builds its own answer carries told, or the caller says a generic "failed"). Every edit the app makes to one character's card runs it. fields are dot paths as PATCH /characters/{id} takes them (null deletes); rootFields carries the library's own flags on the character (fav, mediaLocalized, loreDismissed), which are not card fields; source names the surface that wrote, for the app's own listeners. When the app runs it, char also carries the app's object for that character (null when the app writes by id): read it, never write it. A wrapper that changes the args hands the rest on (next({ ...args, fields })): one rebuilt from scratch drops rootFields, and a flag write then lands nowhere
character.update-many{ updates: [{ id, fields }] }{ affected, failed } (failed names each entry the server refused, with its words). The updates go to the server in batches, in order: a batch the server refuses whole, or that never lands, stops the run there and throws with the server's words (or the reason it did not land) and how many were written before it, the batches before it staying written and read back, and the error's landed holding their { affected, failed }. The app's batch edits run it (the update check's Apply All, the bulk auto-link, the DataCat relink, the Tags Manager's undo of a tag run), and the characters it changed are read back as stored
character.export{ ids, format, worlds } or { filter, format, worlds }By ids: { exported, skipped } by id. By filter: counts, { count, skippedCount }. format is png (the default: a file per character, carrying the card the server holds), charx (a file per character, the card as V3 with its picture and every gallery file; importing one back gives the card as it was stored), links (the provider pages of the linked ones, copied to the clipboard as one list; the rest are skipped) or bundle (one .zip of the cards, their chats and gallery media, and their lorebooks unless worlds is false; it answers its size in bytes too, and by ids a character the server lacks refuses the whole bundle). signal stops it (between files for cards), and onProgress is called with how many characters are done after each
character.import{ file }, { url }, { provider, id } or { stage } (a card file staged with ctx.imports.stage, handed as its stage answered), with onExistingthe import result: success, the character's id and characterName, and for a card already in the library alreadyPresent with the server's skippedReason (identical, diverged-uuid or diverged-provenance; id is then the character it matched). One source a run: file is a card file (a File or Blob: a PNG card, or a charx, whose other images join the character's gallery, counted in the result's mediaCount), url a provider's character page or a plain link to a card file, provider a provider's id with the character's id there (data, what you already fetched from that provider, and options, the provider's own, ride along). It asks nothing: onExisting: 'copy' brings a duplicate in as a new character, and onExisting: { replace: id } writes the card onto that character instead (its id, chats, tags, gallery, lorebook link, favorite and update lock stay, its card and picture become the new card's, the card it had is saved in its versions, and the answer carries replaced: true). A character it brings in shows in the library at once. For a card that came in, the answer also carries what the Import Characters window's download phases read: embeddedMediaUrls, lorebookMediaUrls and galleryPageUrls, and linkedProvider, providerCharId, fullPath and hasGallery when a provider link came with it (a wrapper that builds its own answer carries them, or no download runs). Every provider's import button and creator download runs it as { provider, id }
character.create{ card, image }{ id, name }: a new character from card (a whole card with data, a V2 card when its spec is left off, or the data alone; name is required, at most 128 characters) and image (an ArrayBuffer, a typed array or a Blob of any picture the browser can read, kept at its own size; a placeholder when there is none). It never lands on a character already in the library, and the library shows the new one at once
character.snapshot{ id, label }{ snapshot, label }: the character's card as it is now, kept in its version history under the label (a dated one when none is given) and shown by that character's open history. The Versions tab's Save Snapshot runs it, with char carrying the app's object; the snapshots the app takes on its own before an edit or a restore do not
library.search{ query }true; puts the query in the library's search box and runs it, switching to the characters view first. Typing in the box on the characters view runs it once the box's wait runs out
library.filter{ tag }, { creator } or { playlist }true; exactly one of the three, or it throws, as an empty tag does. tag shows the characters with that tag and clears the other filters, creator searches by creator, playlist filters by a playlist's id (null clears it)
library.select{ ids, value }, { all: true } or { clear: true }how many characters are selected. ids selects them (value: false deselects), all selects every character the view's query matches, clear empties the selection; selecting turns selection mode on. all holds the view's filter, never its ids, and answers the server's count of it; while it holds, ids with value: false leaves those characters out and value: true takes them back, and an id outside the view's set is never added. A new search, chip, rule or playlist filter ends a Select All. A card clicked in selection mode, Select All and Clear Selection run it, and so does leaving selection mode, which empties the selection through clear; a character deleted while selected simply leaves it. Read the selection a verb sends with CoreAPI.getSelection(): { count, ids } or, for a Select All, { count, filter }
tags.apply{ ids, add, remove } or { filter, add, remove }the names in add put on every character and the names in remove taken off; a change the server refuses throws in the server's words. By ids, in one step: { affected, skipped, deltas }, deltas holding what actually changed on each (a tag a character already carried is no change) and skipped the ids the library lacks; the changed characters are re-read. By filter (a GET /api/characters query string; CoreAPI.currentViewFilter() is the library's current view): the server resolves the set (a parameter the list does not take is refused by name, so a misspelled filter never names the whole library) and the command answers once all of it is written, counting what changed, { changed, added, removed } with a count per name (stopped: true when the reader stopped it partway, what landed staying); the library is then refreshed. Batch tagging runs it. Names go as given, so tags you bring in from outside pass the author's import rules first (ctx.CoreAPI.applyTagAliases), as every import does
settings.open{ section }opens Settings, on that section when given, and answers true once it is on screen; an unknown section throws. The app's own jumps into a section run it (an empty library's SillyTavern import, a provider's account section); the gear button does not
view.switch{ view }switches to characters, chats, online or an extension's view (ext:<id>/<view id>, see "A view of your own"); an unknown view throws. Every view change the reader asks for runs it, a conversation's Minimize included; a view the app moves to on its own (a filter's jump to Characters, a removed view sending the reader home) does not
lorebook.open{ name, uid }the lorebook manager opens: on the book called name when you give one, and with uid too (a number, and only with name), that entry opened, scrolled to and briefly marked. true when what you asked for is on screen; false for a book or an entry that is not there (the manager opens on its list, or on the book, and says so) or when the reader chose to keep unsaved edits to the book already open
// your own verb: registered as "my-extension.summarize"
ctx.commands.register('summarize', async ({ chatId }) => {
    /* ... */
    return summary;
}, { title: 'Summarize a chat' });

// anyone can run it by id
const summary = await ctx.commands.run('my-extension.summarize', { chatId });

// wrap a command: log every chat that opens through it, then hand on unchanged
const unwrap = ctx.commands.wrap('chat.open', (next, args) => {
    ctx.log('opening', args.chatId);
    return next();
});

// or change what it is handed: whichever way a delete comes, a character tagged "keep" stays.
// Read the characters from the server: the page's cache may not hold every id you are handed,
// and an id it lacks would read as untagged. A Select All comes as a filter (never its ids), so
// the filter is narrowed with a rule the server applies to every character of the set
ctx.commands.wrap('character.delete', async (next, args) => {
    if (args.filter !== undefined) {
        return next({ ...args, filter: ctx.CoreAPI.narrowFilter(args.filter, { field: 'tags', op: 'excludes', value: 'keep' }) });
    }
    const chars = await Promise.all(args.ids.map(id => ctx.CoreAPI.ensureCharacterById(id)));
    return next({ ids: args.ids.filter((id, i) => !(chars[i]?.tags || []).includes('keep')) });
});

A wrapper runs before the command and decides what happens next: next() hands on with the same args, next(newArgs) with different ones, and a wrapper that returns without calling next answers on its own (an override; pass { override: true } to say so, and when two extensions declare one on the same command, Settings > Extensions shows the conflict under Inspect). Higher priority runs first, then registration order.

  • A wrapper that throws or rejects is skipped and the command carries on without it, so a wrapper that breaks can never take one of the app's verbs down. A wrapper written for { ids } that throws on a { filter } is skipped the same way, so a protection that must hold for every write belongs in a store guard (store.before.character.deleted), which every batch of a by-filter delete passes.
  • A wrapper that never settles holds the verb for whoever ran it, since nothing times it out: hand on or answer, and never wait on something that may not come.
  • A command of your own that throws, or answers a promise that rejects, answers undefined to its caller and logs. An error the command itself raises (one of the app's commands refusing its args, say) passes through your wrapper to whoever ran it, unless you catch it, and is never counted against your extension.

Actions in the menus

A verb that belongs in one of the app's menus is one registration; the host draws the row on desktop and puts it in the matching phone sheet.

ctx.ui.addAction('chat', {
    id: 'summarize',                // [a-z][a-z0-9-]{0,31}, unique within the slot
    label: 'Summarize This Chat',   // up to 40 characters, in Title Case
    icon: 'fa-wand-magic-sparkles',
    when: ({ chatId }) => !!chatId, // optional: hide the row when it does not apply
    run: ({ chatId, character }) => { /* the verb */ },
});
SlotWhere the row appearsWhat run and when receive
chatthe conversation's options menu (the ☰ beside the composer), under an Extensions heading; the phone's options sheet{ chatId, character }
messagea message row's overflow (the ellipsis on the row); the phone's message sheet{ chatId, character, seq, messageId, message }: the row's place, the message's own id and the stored row
characterthe character context menu (right-click or long-press a card), behind its own separator; the phone's sheet{ character }
appthe topbar's ⋮ menu under an Extensions heading; the phone's More sheet{ view } (characters, chats, online or an extension's view)
bulka multi-selection's context menu (right-click a selected card) and the multi-select toolbar, on desktop and phone{ characters, selection }: selection is { count, filter }, filter a Select All's (null for characters picked by hand). run gets the selected characters, read at the click (the list row for each one the page has loaded, { id, fav } for the rest; read anything else by id); when gets them for a hand-picked selection and null under a Select All (decide from selection, never from a list)
chatCarda chat's menu in the chats view (right-click or long-press a chat, or its ⋮), above Delete; the phone's sheet{ chatId, charId, chat }: the chat's id, its character's id (null for a chat with none) and the chat as the list shows it (id, name, charId, charName, messageCount, lastMesAt). There is no character object: read the character by charId when the verb needs it (CoreAPI.getCharacterById(charId) || await CoreAPI.ensureCharacterById(charId))
composera button in a strip just above the conversation's composer, your label its tooltip, your buttons together with a hairline between one extension's and the next's; the buttons that do not fit wait, labelled, behind the strip's More. The same strip on the phone, at the phone's touch size. The strip shows only while a button does{ chatId, character, anchor }: anchor is the button, so a verb can open a menu of its own beside it (an overlay, see "An overlay of your own"); picked from the More, it is the More. when is handed the button it would show as
lorean open lorebook entry: its editor in the lorebook manager, and its read view in a chat's Lorebooks window{ world, uid, entry, chatId }: the book's name, the entry's number, the entry as that surface shows it (uid, comment, key, keysecondary, content, constant, disable, position, order; in the manager that includes edits not saved yet) and the chat whose window it is, null in the manager

A label reads in Title Case, as the app's own menu items and buttons do: every word capitalised but a, an, the, and, but, or, nor, for, to, of, in, on, at, by, from, with, as and unless (unless first or last), so a row of yours sits beside the app's without a second casing. The buttons in your own windows follow the same rule; running text, hints and toasts stay in sentence case.

when is asked every time the menu opens, with the context of that moment (in the app slot, the view the menu opens on; in the bulk slot, the selection, and again while the toolbar shows on every change to it and whenever an extension's setting is written, through ctx.settings.set or in another tab; in the composer slot, as the strip draws: when a conversation opens, when its character arrives, when a composer action is added or removed, and when an extension's setting is written, through ctx.settings.set or in another tab). It must decide synchronously: a when that throws hides its row, and so does one that returns a promise (the console says so once). A run that throws, or returns a promise that rejects, shows a toast naming your extension and the verb and logs to the console. Message actions do nothing while a reply is streaming, like the row's own verbs. A lore entry's verbs are asked for only as an entry opens, and a verb registered or removed while entries are open lands in them at once. Registering with an unknown slot, a bad id, a missing label or icon, or a duplicate id in the same slot throws with the reason.

A badge on a grid card

A chip on every character card in the grid, say a rating, is one registration. render receives the card's row, the same object the grid shows, including the values your server half's projection fields put on it (row.ext['my-extension'].rating), and answers a chip or null:

ctx.ui.contribute('character.card.badge', {
    id: 'rating',                   // [a-z][a-z0-9-]{0,31}, unique within your extension
    render: (row) => {
        const r = row.ext?.['my-extension']?.rating;
        return r == null ? null : { icon: 'fa-star', label: `${r}/5`, tone: 'accent' };
    },
});
  • The chip is { icon, label, tone }: a Font Awesome solid name, a label of up to 24 characters (longer is cut), and one of neutral (the default), accent, success, warning, danger. A chip needs an icon or a label; an icon that is not a Font Awesome name is dropped.
  • A card shows at most three chips, under its favorite star, in the order extensions registered them.
  • render runs while the grid builds its cards, so it must be pure and synchronous: it reads the row and returns. One that throws shows no chip and is named in the console; so is one that returns a promise or anything but an object or null.
  • A card keeps its chip while the grid shows the same result, even as it scrolls away and back. The grid asks again whenever it re-reads its rows from the server: a new search, filter or sort, and every character change the change feed brings in, your own writes reported through ctx.report included. A change this tab patches onto a card in place (the favorite star) does not ask again. Switching your extension on re-asks the grid at once, so your projection arrives with your chip.

A tab in the character details

A tab of your own in the character details window, after the app's tabs:

ctx.ui.contribute('character.details.tab', {
    id: 'notes',                    // [a-z][a-z0-9-]{0,31}, unique within your extension
    label: 'Notes',                 // up to 24 characters
    icon: 'fa-note-sticky',         // optional
    render: async (pane, { character, isCurrent }) => {
        const { text } = await ctx.server.call(`/notes/${character.id}`);   // your server half's route
        if (!isCurrent()) return;   // the details moved on while this was loading
        pane.textContent = text || 'No notes yet.';
    },
});
  • render paints the pane the first time the tab is shown for a character, and again for the next character the window opens on; switching away and back within one character does not ask again. Every open empties your pane first, so it never shows the character before.
  • isCurrent() turns false once the window opens another character; check it after anything you await.
  • The tab behaves like the app's own, the phone's swipe between tabs included. When your extension is switched off, a reader who was on it lands on Details.
  • A render that throws, or returns a promise that rejects, shows a toast naming your extension and the tab and logs to the console. Registering with a bad id, a missing or long label, an icon that is not a Font Awesome name, or a duplicate id throws with the reason.

Events

ctx.events.on(name, fn) listens to the app's listed events; fn is a function, or an object with a handleEvent method as addEventListener takes. fn receives the payload, a frozen object that names WHAT changed by id and never carries the thing itself: re-read it through CoreAPI or the HTTP API. Every listener is handed the same payload, so its lists and objects are frozen too: copy one ([...ids].sort()) before you change it. An event costs nothing while nobody listens to it.

The data events come from the server's change feed, so they fire whatever made the change: this tab, another tab or device signed in to the same account, a script calling the HTTP API, another extension's server half. origin is 'self' when this tab made it and 'remote' for anything else; what a server half writes (through its own routes or its listeners) is 'remote' even in the tab that called the route. By the time your listener runs, the app has already brought its own copy up to date.

ctx.events.on('character.updated', ({ ids, fields, origin }) => {
    if (!fields.includes('tags')) return;
    for (const id of ids) {
        const character = ctx.CoreAPI.getCharacterById(id);
        /* ... */
    }
});
EventPayloadFires when
character.created{ ids, origin }characters were imported or created, their tags and links with them (no character.updated follows for those)
character.updated{ ids, fields, all, origin }characters were written: a save, a tag change, a favorite, an avatar swap (fields names what changed, avatar among them; all is true when the write spanned more characters than a record names, the whole library or a set past a thousand; ids is then empty, so re-read what you hold)
character.deleted{ ids, origin }characters were deleted, and with them their chats, media and links. A delete by filter (a Select All's) is announced in parts, each with origin: 'remote', in the tab that ran it too
chat.created{ ids, charId, branchOf, origin }a chat was started; branchOf is { chatId, seq, messageId } (the message it ends on, by its place and its id) when it is a branch of another chat, null otherwise
chat.updated{ ids, fields, origin }a chat's own row changed: its name, its persona, its draft, its metadata
chat.deleted{ ids, charId, origin }a chat was deleted
message.created{ chatId, seqs, messageIds, origin }messages were added to a chat (seqs are their places, messageIds their own ids, one beside each)
message.updated{ chatId, seqs, messageIds, all, origin }messages were edited, swiped or continued (all when the whole transcript was replaced: seqs and messageIds are then empty)
message.deleted{ chatId, seqs, messageIds, origin }messages were deleted (later places renumber, their ids kept, so re-read the chat)
settings.changed{ keys, all, origin }settings were saved (all for a wholesale save)
playlists.changed{ ids, all, origin }playlists were created, edited, deleted or had members added or removed (all for a whole replace)
tags.changed{ names, origin }a tag was renamed, merged or deleted everywhere (a character's own tags changing is character.updated with tags in fields)
media.changed{ ids, charId, origin }a character's gallery media was added, removed or re-sourced
library.changed{ reason, origin }anything may have changed and nothing is itemized: an import in another process (reason: 'import'), or this tab reconnecting after it lost the feed (reason: 'resync'); re-read what you hold
generation.started{ chatId, mode, targetSeq }a reply (or an Impersonate draft) began streaming; mode is append, regenerate, swipe, continue or impersonate, targetSeq the row a rewrite streams into
generation.ended{ chatId, mode, seq, messageId }the reply was stored at seq as the message messageId (both null for an Impersonate draft, which is never stored), after its row settled on screen
generation.failed{ chatId, mode, cancelled, error }the reply stopped: cancelled when the reader stopped it (then error is null), or it failed or the connection dropped
conversation.changed{ chatId, charId }the open conversation changed: opened, switched or closed (chatId is null on close), and again with the charId once a chat's character, still loading when it opened, resolves
lorebook.changed{ name, origin }a lorebook or a binding to one changed (name is null when several did, or a binding moved)
persona.changed{ scope, personaId, chatId, origin }the persona in use changed: global (the account's active persona; personaId is the one now active, null for none), chat (one chat's own; chatId names it and personaId is the persona it now uses, its override or else the active one) or delete (personaId is the persona deleted, which no chat uses after it). chatId is null but for chat
library.ready{}the library grid's first query answered, once per page load: at startup when the start page is Characters (usually before extensions load, so read the library through CoreAPI rather than waiting for it), otherwise on the reader's first visit to Characters, and never in a session that does not open it
extensions.ready{}every extension enabled at startup finished loading (or failed), once per page load; one switched on later has missed it
ui.formFactorChanged{ mobile }the layout switched between desktop and phone
notifications.changed{ level, visible }the notifications button's state changed (once per change, however often the button repaints behind it)

The three generation events come from this tab's own conversation and name the chat the reply belongs to, even when the reader has moved to another chat by then; a reply another tab or device generates reaches you as message.created instead. The last seven relay one of the app's DOM events, and api 1's names for those still work: ctx.events.on('cl-worlds-changed', fn) adds a plain DOM listener whose fn receives the Event, with the old detail shape (world instead of name, the whole character on cl-chat-changed). cl-character-changed still fires for the app's own surfaces, but only for some writes; character.updated is the complete one. Any other name you pass is such a DOM listener too, which is how you hear another extension's ext:<id>:<name> events. The app's other DOM events are its internal wiring and can change without notice. Every listener you add is removed when your extension is switched off, and ctx.events.off removes one sooner.

Conversation hooks

ctx.hooks.add(name, fn, { priority }) joins one of the conversation's pipelines, and ctx.hooks.list() names them. Hooks run highest priority first, each call contained: one that throws is stepped over (a filter's input passes on to the next one), and every call is timed in the inspector.

HookKindYou getYou answer
message.renderfilterthe message body as HTML, already through the chat's display regex scripts and the app's sanitizer, with comments and tags HTML does not have hidden (their text kept; message is the row as stored); ids come prefixed user-content- and a message's own data-* attributes come renamed data-user-* (so none can carry the name of an app control's attribute), and when the message carried a <style> its top-level elements carry a cl-msg-scope-… class its scoped CSS is keyed to (keep it on what you return), and { chatId, charId, seq, messageId, message, format } (messageId is null for a reply still streaming)the HTML to show (undefined leaves it)
message.enhanceaction{ chatId, seq, messageId, el, message }, el being the message's row in the pagenothing
composer.sendfilter that may waitthe text the reader is sending, and { chatId, charId }the text to post, or '' to take it yourself (nothing is posted and the box clears); a promise is awaited
ctx.hooks.add('message.render', (html, { message }) => {
    const note = message.extra?.['my-ext']?.note;
    return note ? `${html}<div class="myext-note">${ctx.CoreAPI.escapeHtml(note)}</div>` : undefined;
});
ctx.hooks.add('message.enhance', ({ el }) => {
    if (el.querySelector('.myext-toggle')) return; // enhance runs again on a row it has seen
    const button = document.createElement('button');
    /* ... */
});
  • A render filter answers synchronously and with text: a promise, or anything that is not a string, is ignored and counted as an error. It runs when a row is built (a message stored, edited or swiped, a chat opened), never on the paints of a reply that is still streaming: that reply's row is built once it is stored.
  • What you answer goes through the sanitizer again, so buttons, scripts, event handlers and styles come out. Anything interactive goes in with message.enhance, which hands you the row once it is in the page.
  • format(text) renders text the way the row renders its own message (markdown and the speech registers, embedded media pointed at its copies on disk, and the text as stored: a {{char}} or {{user}} in it stays as written, as it does in the transcript) and through the sanitizer. Use it for message text you add, such as a translation or a rewritten reply; escape anything else.
  • message.enhance runs on every path that puts a row in the page, the same row again among them, so check before you add.
  • A message's thinking that is closed has no body in the page until the reader first opens it, unless the reader turned on Render closed thinking up front in Chat settings; opening it does not run message.enhance again. The thinking's text is always in message.extra.reasoning.
  • message is a frozen copy of the stored message. To keep data on a message, write it to extra.<your id> (the message route merges extra key by key, and a server half writes it through the chats store); the app rebuilds the row when the change arrives.
  • composer.send runs when the reader sends a message, and for every chat.send (not for Impersonate or a guided reply, whose text is direction, not a message). It may take its time: the box shows "Preparing your message" and stays read-only until every filter answered, and a reader who moved to another chat meanwhile sends nothing. A filter that throws or rejects leaves the text as typed.
  • Adding or removing a render or enhance hook rebuilds the open conversation's rows once. A conversation with no hooks added pays nothing for them.
  • While any composer.send filter is added, every send waits on it, even when it answers at once: the box goes read-only behind "Preparing your message" for that moment. So join a hook only while it has work and leave it when it has none (ctx.hooks.add answers the removal); the Hello World tour and the Translator both work this way.

Changing how the app looks

  • A message's body is yours through message.render: you may answer a whole new body built from message and format, and add what is interactive with message.enhance.
  • A grid card carries your data as badges (A badge on a grid card).
  • The look of anything is a stylesheet's: your style entry loads into the module layer, below the user's theme settings and custom CSS, and an extension may be a stylesheet alone: a theme of the app's :root tokens needs no code at all. A theme that changes with the reader (palettes they pick, colours read from a picture, a background that moves) adds a client half, and a server half for anything it keeps, as the bundled Midnight does.
  • Restyle at the app's own weight. Your sheet loads after the app's own, so a rule spelling the app's selector wins the tie and still loses to the reader's custom CSS, which loads after yours. A rule weighted above the app's (an extra class, an id, a :not(...), !important) beats the reader's custom CSS too. Scope a rule of your own with :where(...), which adds no weight. Where the app's own rule is !important, answer it with !important on the same selector and property, and nowhere else; the reader's custom CSS still wins the tie.
  • A theme's own choices. Write one class a choice on the root from your client (my-theme-nav-top) and scope each rule in :where(...) (:where(.my-theme-nav-top) .topbar { ... }), so the rule keeps the app selector's weight. Key your default on the other value's absence (:where(:root:not(.my-theme-nav-top)) .topbar): your sheet loads before your client runs, so the default then paints from the first frame instead of flashing. A layout that moves the page's edge changes the width the character grid measured; ask for a new query with CoreAPI.refreshCharacters() once you have moved it.
  • The accent is written by the app on every load, from the reader's theme, at :root and after your sheet, so :root { --accent: ... } in your sheet never shows. A theme that brings an accent declares it under a class it puts on the root (:root.my-theme-accent { --accent: ...; --accent-rgb: ...; }) and sets that class only while the reader has picked no accent of their own: their highlightColor setting equal to CoreAPI.getDefaultSetting('highlightColor') and no --accent in their customCSS setting. Follow settings.changed for both keys.
  • Hiding. An element carrying the hidden attribute is hidden whatever display your sheet gives it (the app's [hidden] rule is !important); toggle el.hidden, and give a display: ... !important of your own a :where(:not([hidden])), or it shows a hidden element.

There is no door to swap one of the app's own components for yours (a grid card's markup, a details tab's body, a chat card): the app keeps their structure, and you reach them through the slots, hooks and styles above.

Beyond the stable surface

Nothing is fenced, but not everything is within reach. What you can reach beside ctx's doors: the whole HTTP API (ctx.CoreAPI.apiRequest), the page's DOM, and the few bridges the app publishes on window (ExtensionHost, chatsModule, ProviderRegistry). What you cannot: the private insides of the app's ES modules, such as the Chats view's own functions, which reach you only through CoreAPI, commands and those bridges. More doors are on their way as registered surfaces: more commands, slots and hooks across every part of the app. The deal is symmetric: anything outside ctx may change in any release without notice, and so may what a CoreAPI function takes and answers (its name stays, or is listed below first), so expect to maintain your extension. requires.cl is reserved for pinning the Project Σ version you tested against and is not enforced yet.

A list the HTTP API answers that grows with the library pages: a request naming no limit reads its first page (the default its x-collection names, 100 for most), never the whole list, so a reader that needs all of it walks nextCursor with after. ctx.CoreAPI.fetchAllPages(path, params) does the walk.

Two rules of the house worth honoring even though nothing enforces them on you:

  • Third-party text rendered with innerHTML goes through the app's sanitizer paths. If you render content from an external API, look at how the browse views use safePurify before rolling your own.
  • Settings and storage are per user. Do not stash per-user data in localStorage without scoping it; the app's own code never does.

Deprecated doors

From the first public release on, a door on its way out is listed here, with what replaces it, one release before it leaves: a command, event, hook or slot, a ctx member, a CoreAPI function, a server ctx member, a store guard or a pipeline. Nothing is listed yet.

The HTTP API marks its own in its specification (deprecated in /api/openapi.json, and a list's form on its way out in the list's x-collection), on the same rule.

The server half

An extension can ship server code beside its client: add "server": "server/index.js" to the manifest. The entry is an ES module exporting register(router, ctx); it runs once at server boot, and its routes mount under /api/ext/<your-id>/ behind the same sign-in gate as the rest of the API, so every request arrives with req.db, req.userHandle and req.paths scoped to the requesting user exactly as first-party routes see them. Your routes answer only the accounts that switched your extension on; any other account gets a 404.

export function register(router, ctx) {
    router.get('/status', (req, res) => res.json({ user: req.userHandle }));
    router.post('/rebuild', ctx.requireAdmin, (req, res) => { /* admins only */ });
}

The server ctx:

FieldWhat it is
ctx.apiVersionthe extension api this server speaks, 'major.minor'; a manifest whose requires.api is newer is refused (see "Anatomy") and its half is never imported
ctx.manifestyour manifest row
ctx.requireAdminroute middleware; gates a route to admins
ctx.requireChatroute middleware for a route whose path names :chatId: it answers 404 { error: 'no such chat' } unless that chat is in the account's library, so the handler reads req.params.chatId knowing it is there
ctx.multiUserwhether this server runs with accounts (false on single-user installs)
ctx.settingValue(db, key, fallback)read a user's setting (pass req.db)
ctx.setting(db, key)read your extension's OWN per-user config: the scoped key plus your manifest-declared default, the same declaration the client facade reads, so the two halves cannot drift. Use settingValue only for first-party keys
ctx.chatSlot.get(db, chatId) / set(db, chatId, value)your own slot in a chat's metadata (chat_metadata.extensions.<your-id>), which travels with the chat into its export and a branch: get answers it ({} when there is none), set replaces it (null removes it), leaving the chat's other keys and every other extension's slot as they are; a chat that is not there throws (statusCode 404). A set is reported as a change to the chat's chatMetadata, which every write of the metadata reports (each reply among them), so a client half that keeps a copy of its slot has the write report a field of its own beside it (ctx.report in the same transaction) and follows that
ctx.messages.shown(msg) / digest(text)the text a stored message shows and its digest, as the page's ctx.messages answers them; store the digest beside what you made of a message and the page can tell when the message changed
ctx.debugLog(req, ...)routine logging behind that user's debug toggle, tagged [ext:<id>]; use console.error/warn for failures
ctx.browser.consumer({ name, origin, fetchPrefix, warmPath, ready })a hosted-browser consumer of your own, for a Cloudflare-gated site: a bare https origin and the path prefix (starting and ending with /) your page's ctx.browser.fetch stays under, warmPath where its parked page waits (default /), ready the settle hook (default the Cloudflare wait; null for none). name is required: lowercase letters, digits and dashes, starting with a letter, at most 32. Registered as <your id>:<name>, which no built-in consumer can take, and answers that id. The browser routes serve it only for accounts that switched you on (any other gets a 404), and it goes with your half: once your half fails or you are removed there is no such consumer, and a request naming it answers 400. Settings > Online > Hosted Browser lists it under Extension Sites with a Check. The browser carries every login it holds on that site, so keep the prefix to what you need
ctx.helpersthe hardened transport helpers the first-party provider services run on: guardProxyTarget (the proxy security gate), sessionSlot (a per-user in-memory credential slot), sameHostFetch (redirect-pinned fetch), wildcardPath
ctx.dataDir()extension-global scratch dir (caches) at extensions/installed/.data/<id>/, never served and never inside your install; removing the extension deletes it
ctx.userDataDir(req)a per-user directory inside that user's own data home; removing the extension deletes it from every account's home
ctx.files.user(req) / shared()the two folders above as handles: user(req) the requesting account's, shared() the one every account shares. Each answers read(name) (the text, null when the file is not there), readJson(name, fallback) (the file parsed; when it is not there the fallback, or null without one; a file that is not JSON throws, naming it), write(name, data) (a string or a Buffer), writeJson(name, value) (pretty-printed), remove(name) (a file, or a folder and everything in it; nothing there is no error), list(sub) (the names in the folder or a folder inside it, [] when it is not there) and path(name) (the absolute path, for something that opens a real file). Every name is a path relative to the folder (cache/index.json): an absolute path, a drive, a .. that leaves the folder or a name not every disk can hold (aux.json, a:b) throws, naming it. A write lands whole, through a temporary file renamed over the old one, with its folders made first. Synchronous, for small files: hand a large one to a stream of your own through path(name). See "Where to store what"
ctx.storesthe app's own store modules, the functions its routes call (ctx.stores.characters, ctx.stores.chats, ctx.stores.tags and the rest); see "Writing to the library"
