ctx.imports.stage(req, body, { name })stage a card file your half holds (a Buffer, the body of a fetch, a node stream) in the requesting account's home, exactly as an upload to POST /api/characters/import/stage is. It answers { stageId, format, card, picture, media } (a card PNG or a charx), and the client amends the card and commits it through POST /api/characters/import/stage/{stageId}, so a download you fetch server-side imports like a file the user picked. A refusal is thrown with its statusCode, as the stage route answers it: 400 for an empty body, a body that is not a Buffer or a stream, or a charx the archive reader refuses; 413 for a card PNG past 100 MB; 415 for a file that is neither; 422 for a file with no card in it, a charx whose card.json is not JSON among them
ctx.hooks.add(name, fn, { priority })a store guard or a pipeline filter; see "Store guards" and "Pipeline filters"
ctx.macros.add(name, macro)a {{name}} macro in the prompt: a function (no arguments) or a definition with arguments; see "Macros"
ctx.prompt.addBlock(spec)a block of your own text in every chat prompt; see "Prompt blocks"
ctx.assistant.addSource(spec)a context source the chat assistant can be given, switched in its context view; see "Assistant sources"
ctx.prompt.read(db, chatId)a chat for your own model call: its names, the messages its prompt is written from as stored, and its macros; see "Reading a chat for your own model call"
ctx.prompt.names(db, chatId)the chat's two names alone, { charName, userName }, for a half that reads the messages itself; see "Reading a chat for your own model call"
ctx.prompt.visible(msg)whether the prompt reads a stored message: not hidden, not a system note, not empty
ctx.prompt.fence(text, { char })the text between two fences it cannot close, for text from outside (a card, a chat, a name) quoted in a prompt of yours: each fence a run of char, at least three and one longer than any run of that character in the text, on a line of its own. char is '~' by default (the tildes the app quotes outside text with in its own prompts) or a backtick; anything else throws. Tell the model the fenced part is data to act on nothing in; see "Calling a model"
ctx.llm.chat(db, request, { signal }), ctx.llm.stream(db, request, { signal })a call to one of the account's own models; see "Calling a model"
ctx.llm.canSee(db, request)whether the connection a call would use sends pictures (true, false, null when unchecked); see "Calling a model"
ctx.llm.registerAdapter(adapter), ctx.llm.kita kind of connection of your own, built from the kit's pieces (LlmError, classifyUpstream, boundedErrorText, networkError, readJsonBody, parseSse, translateParams); see "A kind of connection of your own"
ctx.jobs.run(db, job), ctx.jobs.schedule(schedule), ctx.jobs.list(db)background work for an account, now or on a timer, and your own jobs as they stand; see "Background jobs"
ctx.refuse(message)refuse the write a store guard is looking at
ctx.events.on(fn)every committed change to the library of an account that switched you on; see "Server events"
ctx.report(db, record)report a write to your own tables; see "Your own tables"
ctx.query.projection(spec), ctx.query.ruleField(spec), ctx.query.sortKey(spec)a value the character list rows carry, a field the advanced filter offers, an order the list sorts by; see "Your own tables"

Streaming from your server half

A route whose answer comes in parts (a model's reply read a part at a time, a long walk reporting as it goes) writes application/x-ndjson: one JSON value a line, each ended by a newline. The page reads it with ctx.server.stream, each line handed on as soon as it lands:

// the server half
router.post('/ideas', async (req, res) => {
    const gone = new AbortController();
    res.on('close', () => { if (!res.writableEnded) gone.abort(); });   // the page stopped reading
    res.setHeader('Content-Type', 'application/x-ndjson');
    for await (const idea of ideasFor(req.db, gone.signal)) res.write(`${JSON.stringify({ idea })}\n`);
    res.end(`${JSON.stringify({ done: true })}\n`);
});

// the page
const stop = new AbortController();
for await (const line of ctx.server.stream('/ideas', 'POST', { about: 'the river' }, { signal: stop.signal })) {
    if (line.idea) show(line.idea);
}
  • Refuse before you write the first line, with a status and { error } as any route does: the page's loop throws in your words. A failure after the first line can only be a line of its own (say { error }), which the page reads like any other.
  • An answer that is not NDJSON (a plain res.json(...)) is handed to the loop once, so one route can answer either way.
  • The page stopping (its signal, or leaving the loop) closes the request: listen for close on the response and stop the work, a model call included (ctx.llm.stream takes your signal).

Your own tables

Two places hold what your extension keeps about a chat or a message. What should travel with the chat, into its export and a branch, rides the row: a message's extra.<your id>, and a chat's metadata under extensions.<your id> (ctx.chatSlot, or ctx.stores.chats.patchChat(db, chatId, { chatMetadata: { extensions: { '<your id>': value } } }), which merges your key alone, null removing it). Like every store function it refuses by throwing an error with a statusCode (404 for an unknown chat), which the app's error handler answers when your route lets it through. Anything derived, local or large belongs in your own tables.

A message has an id of its own (chat_messages.id, an integer never handed out again) and a place in its chat (chat_id, seq, 0 to n - 1), and deleting a message moves every later one down a place while its id stays. So:

  • Data ABOUT a message (a translation cache, a rating, an embedding) references chat_messages(id) ON DELETE CASCADE: it stays on its message wherever the message sits and goes with it.
  • A table that walks the transcript in order (a state as of message seq, as Trackers keeps them) references chat_messages(chat_id, seq) with ON DELETE CASCADE ON UPDATE CASCADE, so the renumber carries its rows; a migration that keys on the place without ON UPDATE CASCADE is refused.
  • A whole transcript replaced (PUT /chats/{id}/messages, a re-import over a chat of the same name, a bundle restore) is new messages with new ids: the rows keyed on the old ones cascade away, and rows keyed on the place start over.

Data a character, chat or message owns belongs in tables, not squeezed into a card. Export migrations beside register, and the app builds your tables in the library of each account that switches your extension on (the bundled extensions/bundled/ratings-notes/ is a complete working example: a table, writes that report, and all three query doors):

export const migrations = [
    `CREATE TABLE x_my_extension_ratings (
        char_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES characters(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
        rating INTEGER, updated_at INTEGER NOT NULL);
     CREATE INDEX x_my_extension_ratings_rating ON x_my_extension_ratings(rating);`,
];
  • Each entry is an SQL string or a function (db) => void, run once per library, in order, in a transaction of its own: the moment an account switches you on, or when its library opens with you on. An account that never switched you on never gets your tables. The list is append-only: an entry that already ran is never edited, and an edited one fails your server half by name. Add a new entry instead.
  • Foreign keys are off while an entry runs, so a table you rebuild by the usual recipe (create the new one, copy, drop the old, rename) keeps the rows that reference it. Before the entry commits, every row of your tables must still point at a row that exists; one that does not rolls the entry back and fails your server half by name.
  • Everything you create is named x_<your id>_..., with your id's hyphens as underscores (x_my_extension_). An object named otherwise, one on a table of the app's, a dropped or changed app object, or a changed schema version rolls the entry back and fails your server half for every account, whichever account's library found it.
  • REFERENCES characters(id) ON DELETE CASCADE makes a character's delete take your rows with it.
  • Removing your extension keeps its tables and their data.

A write to your own tables reaches no guard and no open page on its own: report it, inside the same transaction, as the change it is to the app's data (a character's rating is a change to that character), with fields named after your id:

router.put('/rating/:id', (req, res) => {
    req.db.transaction(() => {
        req.db.prepare('INSERT INTO x_my_extension_ratings (char_id, rating, updated_at) VALUES (?, ?, ?) ON CONFLICT(char_id) DO UPDATE SET rating = excluded.rating, updated_at = excluded.updated_at')
            .run(req.params.id, req.body.rating, Date.now());
        ctx.report(req.db, { entity: 'character', op: 'updated', ids: [req.params.id], fields: ['my-extension.rating'] });
    })();
    res.json({ ok: true });
});

The store guards see the report before the write commits, and every open tab of that account re-reads the character after it. A message record also names its chatId, as the app's own do; its ids are the messages' seqs, and it may carry messageIds, the messages' own ids, one beside each seq. The record carries extension: 'my-extension' (the app sets it; you cannot), so whoever reads it can tell a change to your data from a change to the character itself: the character's details, for one, raise no "changed elsewhere" notice over a report of yours. Your own tab, the one that wrote, gets the record too, so a listener that re-reads should repaint only what changed.

Three query doors read your tables inside the app's own character queries, for the accounts that switched you on. Declare them inside register; a door declared after register returned (from a route, a listener or a timer) is refused:

ctx.query.projection({ key: 'rating', table: 'x_my_extension_ratings', column: 'rating' });
ctx.query.ruleField({ key: 'rating', label: 'Rating', type: 'number', table: 'x_my_extension_ratings', column: 'rating' });
ctx.query.sortKey({ key: 'rating', label: 'Rating', table: 'x_my_extension_ratings', column: 'rating' });
  • A projection field rides every character list row as ext['my-extension'].rating (null for a character with no value), so a grid badge needs no request of its own.
  • A rule field is an advanced-filter field named ext.my-extension.rating. Its operations follow its type: number (more_than, less_than, equals), date (before, after, in_the_last), text (equals), boolean (is_true, is_false), and for every type is_set and is_not_set.
  • A sort key is a list sort named ext.my-extension.rating. The characters with a value come first, in the column's order (order=desc reverses them), ties in the order their rows were first written (an upsert keeps its row's place; on a WITHOUT ROWID table, by the character's id); the characters with no value (no row, or NULL) follow by name, A to Z, whatever the order. It pages by offset only, never by cursor. Its table holds at most one row per character (charColumn is its primary key or has a unique index), and a text column sorts by the collation the column declares.
  • charColumn names the column holding the character's id (char_id by default).
  • Each door must be served by an index: a projection by one on charColumn, a rule field by one on column, a sort key by one on column that walks it in order (on a WITHOUT ROWID table, one on column, charColumn). A door no such index serves fails your server half, naming the index to add. The check asks for the index, not the table's size: a table small enough that SQLite scans it today still passes.
  • Your inventory row lists what you declared (serverQuery), so a client offers only what the server will answer.
  • The bundled client adds your sort keys to the grid's sort menu (each one descending and ascending, grouped under your extension's name) and your rule fields to the advanced filter, for as long as the account has you on. Switched off, a sort of yours falls back to the reader's default sort, and a filter rule on your field is removed with a notice; a saved filter preset keeps the rule for when you are back on.

Writing to the library

Write through ctx.stores, passing req.db (or the db a guard or listener is handed):

router.post('/tag-all/:id', (req, res) => {
    ctx.stores.tags.addCharTags(req.db, req.params.id, ['reviewed']);
    res.json({ ok: true });
});

A delete through the stores takes the row's files with it (a character's picture and gallery, a persona's picture, a media file, a background) when you pass the account's paths, req.paths: ctx.stores.characters.deleteCharacter(req.db, req.paths, id). A write through the stores is a write like the app's own: the store guards see it, and every open tab of that account hears it on the change feed and updates. A raw statement on req.db does neither: no guard sees it and no open page learns of it until it re-reads. The store functions follow the server's own releases, so check the changelog when you upgrade.

Store guards

A store guard runs after a write to the library and before it commits, and may refuse it:

ctx.hooks.add('store.before.character.updated', (record, { db, user }) => {
    // a guard runs at the commit of every such write: look at what it touched before reading a card
    if (Array.isArray(record.fields) && !record.fields.includes('name')) return;
    for (const id of record.ids || []) {
        const character = ctx.stores.characters.getCharacter(db, id);
        if (!character?.card?.data?.name?.trim()) ctx.refuse('A character needs a name');
    }
});
  • The name is store.before.<entity>.<op>, one per kind of record the change feed carries (character.updated, message.created, persona.deleted and so on). A name no write ever reports is refused when you register it, with the reason.
  • A write reports each row once. An import is character.created alone, its tags included, so a character.updated guard never sees one; a card save that changes the tags is one character.updated naming tags beside the other fields it changed. A tag add through the stores that links nothing new (the character already carries every tag given) is no write: nothing is stamped and nothing is reported.
  • record is the change record (entity, op, ids or all: true, fields where the write names them, and extension when an extension reported it), frozen. db is that account's library, holding the write that has not committed yet: read it to decide, never write through it.
  • ctx.refuse(message) rolls the whole write back, and the request that made it answers 400 with your message. Any other throw is caught and logged, and the write goes ahead.
  • A guard is synchronous: a write cannot wait. A guard that returns a promise is not waited on and cannot refuse; the server says so in its log, once.
  • Guards run highest priority first (default 0), then in registration order, and only for the accounts that switched your extension on.
  • A guard on a delete reads a library where the row is already gone, and a guard on a picture change (fields: ['avatar']) sees the report, not the new picture.

Pipeline filters

A pipeline filter rewrites a value where the app produces it, for the accounts that switched your extension on:

ctx.hooks.add('card.import', (card, { db, user, sourceName, provider }) => {
    card.data.extensions['my-extension'] = { importedFrom: sourceName };
    return card;
});
namewherevaluealso in the second argument
card.importevery import of a card (a file, a charx, a link, a provider, a bundle), before its identity and its content hash are readthe card, { spec, spec_version, data }sourceName (the file name), provider (the provider it came through, or null)
card.exportevery export of a card (the PNG, the bundle)the card as exportedid
chat.exporta chat's JSONL exportits entries: the header object, then one object per messagechatId, charId
lore.candidatesevery lore scan (the prompt, the chat's lore panel, the assistant's context)books of your own to scan beside the chat's (a list, empty to start): { name, data: { entries } }, each name written ext:<your id>:<label> and each entry carrying a uid and its contentchatId, charId, trigger, dryRun, books (the names of the chat's books in force) and texts (the messages the keys are matched against at the global scan depth, oldest first, as the engine reads them)
lore.activatethe same scans, after the engine placed what firedthe placed entries in five groups, before, after, atDepth, emBefore, emAfter, then anTop and anBottom (the entries wrapping the chat's Author's Note, sent with it only when it is) and outlets (the entries {{outlet::name}} places, each naming its outlet): drop an entry or change its content; an answer without anTop, anBottom or outlets keeps the engine'schatId, charId, trigger, dryRun, and the same books and texts
prompt.finalevery chat prompt once the budget has fitted it: each send, whatever its mode, and the prompt inspectorthe messages, [{ role, content }], the chat's turns already through its regex scripts that rewrite what the prompt sends, each role one of system, user, assistant; a user's turn that shows the model pictures also carries its images, so keep them when you rebuild a message (a turn of the example dialogue is a system message whose text opens with its speaker's name)chatId, charId, trigger (normal, continue, impersonate, swipe or regenerate), dryRun
llm.requestevery call to a model: the chat, the assistant, the studio, a connection test and a raw POST /api/llm/chatthe request, { messages, model, params }: params in the app's own names (temperature, topP, topK, maxTokens, stop, presencePenalty, frequencyPenalty, seed, reasoning, json), model set only when the call chose one over its connection'spurpose (chat, assistant, creator, test, tool or extension), profileKind, profileId, and extension (the calling extension's id) when the purpose is extension; and which chat or session the call is for: chatId and mode on a chat's reply, chatId and sessionId on the assistant's (both rounds of a read), sessionId and charId on the studio's, the same names reply.final is told on that surface (none on the tag wand, a test, a tool call or an extension's)
reply.finalevery reply a model writes that is stored: the chat's (each mode but impersonate, whose text goes to the composer), the assistant's and the studio's, a partial kept after an error or a stop includedthe reply's text; for continue, only the new text, which is then added to the messagesurface (chat, assistant or creator), chatId (null in the studio), interrupted (why a partial was cut short, or null); mode in the chat, sessionId in the assistant and the studio, charId in the studio
  • Return the value to hand on, or undefined to hand on what you were given. Each filter gets its own copy, so change it freely: a filter that throws half way leaves nothing behind.
  • A card must keep what an import relies on: data.name (non-empty text), data.tags (a list of text) and data.extensions (an object). A chat export stays a non-empty list of objects. An answer of the wrong shape is skipped, as a throw is, and the value your filter was given passes on.
  • A filter is synchronous. A promise is ignored (the server says so in its log, once) and the value passes on: the filters run inside the request that writes, where a slow one stalls every request on that account's library. Work that has to wait (a model call, a fetch) belongs after the write, in ctx.events.on.
  • Filters run highest priority first (default 0), each handing its answer to the next. Every call is timed and counted in the inspector, a failure against its pipeline.
  • db is the account's library, for reading. Write nothing through it here: a write belongs to the request's own store calls or to your listener.
  • Your books from lore.candidates fire like any book's entries (keys, order, position, depth, and every setting of their own) and are scanned in the global scope, after the chat's, persona's and character's books. Name each ext:<your id>:<label>: a book named for another extension, or with nothing after your prefix, is the wrong shape (a book another filter handed you passes on under its own name). The chat's Lorebooks window lists your books, read-only, under "Added by extensions"; the prompt inspector shows what they placed. books and texts are frozen; read them rather than reading the chat or its books a second time. dryRun is true in the panel and the assistant (nothing will be sent) and false for a real send. Dropping an entry in lore.activate takes it out of the prompt and the panel alike.
  • prompt.final has the last word on what is sent, after the budget: what you add there is not measured against the connection's context size, so keep it short, or add it as a prompt block, which is. The prompt inspector shows its answer, with dryRun true.
  • llm.request's purpose is set by the route that made the call, never by the request body: a raw POST /api/llm/chat is always tool, and a call through ctx.llm is extension. The scope (chatId, sessionId, charId, mode) is the route's too, so a body naming a chat never sets it; a filter adding a chat's own state to the assistant's material reads chatId rather than guessing from the newest session. Your params meet the rules a caller's own do (a name from the list above, a number where one is due); an answer breaking them is the wrong shape.
  • reply.final changes what is stored, and what the reader sees once the reply lands (the words that stream in before it are the model's own). Answer text that is not blank: a filter cannot delete a reply. Work on a stored reply that has to wait (a summary, a memory) belongs in ctx.events.on, where the stored message arrives as a change.
  • Two consequences to design for. A card card.import rewrites is hashed as rewritten, so a later import of the same file matches the stored character only when your filter answers the same way again. And an ST data directory migrated into the app is imported by a separate process, where no server half runs, so its cards do not pass card.import.

Macros

// {{weather}}: a function alone is a macro that takes no arguments
ctx.macros.add('weather', ({ db, chatId }) => readForecast(db, chatId));
// {{dice::2d20}}: a definition declares its arguments
ctx.macros.add('dice', { args: [{ name: 'formula' }], handler: ({ unnamedArgs: [formula] }) => rollDice(formula) });
  • {{weather}} then expands anywhere a prompt is built from (the preset, the card's fields, lore keys and content), for the accounts that switched your extension on; for any other it stays as written, like an unknown macro.
  • Your handler runs each time a text names the macro, when that text is expanded, as the app's own macros do (two {{roll::d6}} roll twice): a prompt build, the lore panel's scan, a sent turn, a chat's greeting as the chat is created, and ctx.prompt.read's expand. A macro no text names is never called. Keep it cheap, and make it read, never write: a lore key holding it runs it at every scan, and the prompt inspector and the lore panel build prompts that are never sent.
  • It answers text (a number is written out), and the answer goes in as written: it is never expanded again. A throw, a promise or anything else leaves that call as written and counts as an error in the inspector. A handler that expands its own macro again (through ctx.prompt.read) has that inner call left as written.
  • It is handed the build's facts (db, user, chatId, charId, lastSeq) and the call: name, args (every argument, each already resolved, so {{dice::{{getvar::n}}d6}} hands it the variable's value), unnamedArgs (the declared ones), list (the ones after them, or null for a macro without a list) and isScoped.
  • args declares the arguments in order, each { name, type, optional }. type is string (the default), integer, number or boolean, or a list of them. An optional argument comes after every required one. list: true (or { min, max }) takes any number more after them. Arguments are written after :: ({{dice::2d20}}), or one after a single : or a space ({{dice 2d20}}).
  • A call with the wrong count or a mistyped argument stays as written and your handler is not called. strict: false hands it to your handler anyway, to judge args yourself.
  • A macro whose last declared argument can take it may be written as a block: {{shout}}quiet{{/shout}} hands quiet (trimmed) as its last argument, with isScoped true. A macro with a list is never a block.
  • lastSeq is the seq of the newest message the build reads, as a prompt block is told it (below); the lore panel's scan tells your macro the same, so a lore key holding your macro fires there as it does in the prompt. A reader's turn expanded as it is sent is told the newest message the prompt reads before it, never a hidden one.
  • A name is letters and underscores ([a-z][a-z_]{0,31}), never one of the app's own (char, user, description, pick, random and the rest), and never one another extension holds; a definition takes only the keys above (args, list, strict, handler) under the rules above. Any refusal fails your server half at registration, naming the reason.
  • Macros expand in the prompt a reply is built from. A chat's greeting is expanded once, when the chat is created, with the whole vocabulary, yours included (called with no chatId yet), and the answer is stored in the chat's first message, as a sent turn that holds a macro is stored expanded: text that should follow the chat as it goes belongs in the prompt, never in a greeting.

Prompt blocks

ctx.prompt.addBlock({
    id: 'reminder',
    position: 'at_depth', depth: 2, role: 'system',
    text: ({ db, user, chatId, charId, dryRun, lastSeq }) => reminderFor(db, chatId),
});
  • text runs once per chat prompt, for the accounts that switched your extension on, and its answer is the block's text in that prompt. Blank text adds nothing; a throw, a promise or anything but text adds nothing either and counts as an error in the inspector. dryRun is true for the prompt inspector (nothing will be sent) and false for a send.
  • lastSeq is the seq of the newest message of the history the reply is written from: the reader's turn for a send, the message before the target for a swipe or a regenerate (whose target is left out), the target itself for a continue, never a hidden message; null when the history holds none. A block that keeps something per turn answers as of that message, so a swipe is not told the turn it is replacing.
  • position places it: pre_history (the default) after the preset's own blocks, right before the conversation; post_history after the preset's blocks that follow the conversation; at_depth inside the conversation, depth turns back from the newest (0, the default, is after it), beside the lore, the Author's Note and the Character's Note placed there. Blocks sharing a depth are ordered by order (default 100): the higher sits nearer the reply.
  • role is system (the default), user or assistant. Before or after the conversation a block is a message of its own, as each of the preset's blocks is; when the chat's preset has Join neighbouring system blocks on, a system block joins the system messages beside it, as the preset's own do. At a depth, the blocks, lore and notes that share the depth, order and role go as one message (the lore, the Author's Note and the Character's Note sit at order 100).
  • A block the user places (a setting of yours) answers its place with its text: text may return { text, position, depth, role, order } instead of the text alone, each placement field optional (the one you registered stands in for any you leave out), so each prompt puts the block where that account's setting says. A placement outside the rules above adds nothing and counts as an error. For example text: ({ db, chatId }) => ({ text: recapOf(db, chatId), position: ctx.setting(db, 'place') }).
  • The text is expanded like the preset's own ({{char}}, {{user}}, the extensions' macros) and counted by the budget like the rest of the prompt: a long block leaves less room for the conversation.
  • An id is lowercase letters, digits and dashes ([a-z][a-z0-9-]{0,31}), unique among your own blocks. A second block with the same id, or a position or role outside these, fails your server half at registration, naming the reason.
  • Blocks join the chat's prompt only; the assistant and the studio build theirs from their own material. To hand the chat assistant something, register a source (below).

Assistant sources

ctx.assistant.addSource({
    id: 'trackers',
    label: 'Tracked state',
    defaultOn: true,
    read: ({ db, user, chatId, charId }) => trackedStateFor(db, chatId),
});
  • A source is one more thing the chat assistant can be given beside the card, the persona, the Author's Note and the rest. The assistant's context view lists it with a switch, its label and its size, for the accounts that switched your extension on; defaultOn (false when left out) is its state until the reader flips it.
  • read runs once per assistant build (a send, the context view, the prompt preview) while the source is on, and never while it is off. Its answer is the text shared, set among the story material under your label and your extension's name, inside the same quotation as the card: the assistant reads it as data, never as instructions. Blank text shares nothing and the view says so; a throw, a promise or anything but text shares nothing, the view says the source failed, and the inspector counts it as an error.
  • The text is not expanded (no macros) and not capped: share what helps a question, not everything you keep.
  • An id is lowercase letters, digits and dashes ([a-z][a-z0-9-]{0,31}), unique among your own sources; a label is one line of at most 60 characters. A second source with the same id, or a label outside that, fails your server half at registration, naming the reason.

Reading a chat for your own model call

const chat = ctx.prompt.read(db, chatId);
// chat.charName, chat.userName, chat.history: [{ id, seq, msg }], chat.expand(text)
const lines = chat.history.slice(-6).map(({ msg }) => `${msg.name || (msg.is_user ? chat.userName : chat.charName)}: ${msg.mes}`);
const ask = chat.expand('Summarize what {{char}} knows about {{user}}.');
  • charName and userName are the names the chat's prompt uses: the card's name, and the chat's own persona, else the account's, else the name the chat was stored with.
  • history is the messages a reply is written from, oldest first, each { id, seq, msg } (the message's own id, its place, the stored row): no hidden message, no system note, no empty one. Read it when you ask a model about the chat, rather than picking the messages yourself. Each msg is the stored message: the chat's regex scripts that rewrite what the prompt sends are not applied to it, and nothing is left out for length, so with such a script on, the chat's own model reads other words than the ones you send.
  • expand(text) expands the chat's macros as a preset's block is expanded ({{char}}, {{user}}, the card's fields, the history's, the extensions' own), for text of yours the prompt does not hold, such as the instructions of your own model call.
  • It answers null for a chat that does not exist. It reads, and writes nothing.
  • ctx.prompt.names(db, chatId) answers the two names alone, { charName, userName } (or null), for a half that reads the chat's messages itself (ctx.stores.chats.listMessageRows, a page at a time or past what it already holds): read reads every message, so asking it for the names reads the chat a second time.
  • ctx.prompt.visible(msg) is the rule history is drawn by, for one message: whether the prompt reads a stored message (not hidden, not a system note, not empty). Ask it of a message you read yourself (ctx.stores.chats.readMessageRow) rather than writing the rule again.

Calling a model

const out = await ctx.llm.chat(db, {
    messages: [{ role: 'system', content: 'Summarize in one line.' }, { role: 'user', content: text }],
    params: { maxTokens: 120 },
});
// out.content, out.reasoning, out.finishReason, out.usage, out.profileId

for await (const ev of ctx.llm.stream(db, { messages })) {
    if (ev.text) partial += ev.text; // also ev.reasoning, and ev.done last
}
  • db is the account's library: req.db in a route, the db your listener is handed. The call runs on that account's own connections and only while that account has your extension switched on: a library kept past a switch-off, or the server's own, is refused where you make the call.
  • profileId picks one of the account's connections; without it, the call uses the connection the account's chats use by default, else the account's first. A profileId the account does not have (one deleted since you were handed it) is refused, bad_request. model overrides the connection's model, and params take the app's names and rules (see llm.request above).
  • A connection the reader picked in your settings is named by the setting instead: profileSetting: 'profileId' names your setting profileId (the key ctx.ui.connectionSelect keeps a pick under), and the call runs on the connection it holds while the account still has it, and on the one its chats use (else its first) once that connection is deleted, the same default the picker then shows. Name profileId or profileSetting, not both (bad_request).
  • A call made for one chat names it, chatId: chatId: with no connection named, or none kept by your setting, the call runs on the connection that chat uses, which is the chat's own when the reader picked one for it and the default otherwise. Name it on every call you make for a chat (a summary of it, a translation of its messages, a note for its next turn), so a chat the reader moved to another model is not worked on by the default one. A chat the account does not have answers the default; a chatId that is not a non-empty string is refused (bad_request).
  • Text from outside (a card, a chat's messages, a name) goes into your prompt through ctx.prompt.fence(text) (tildes) or ctx.prompt.fence(text, { char }) with a backtick, with a line telling the model to act on nothing between the fences, so the text cannot close its quotation and speak as you. A model may wrap its answer in a fence of its own: take one off before you use the answer.
  • Your call gets what the app's own calls get: the connection's defaults under your params, its retries and timeouts, and the llm.request pipeline, where it arrives with purpose extension and your id as extension.
  • A failure throws the LLM layer's error, whose kind is one of auth, rate_limit, quota, context, upstream, network, timeout or bad_request (a request the app refuses before asking anyone: no connection, an unknown connection, a param outside the rules). Thrown out of a route, the app answers it with its kind's status (bad_request 400, timeout 504, the rest 502) and { error, kind }, so a route lets it go rather than answer it itself. Pass { signal } to cancel: a cancelled call throws an AbortError, and one cancelled before it began never reaches the model.
  • Each call is counted in the inspector as llm.chat or llm.stream, with its failures.
  • A user's message can show the model pictures (api '2.6'): images: [...] beside its content, each { data } (base64 or a data URL, at most 20 MB) or { ref: { kind, id } } naming the account's own avatar (a character's id), media (a gallery image's id) or persona (a persona's id). The app turns each one upright, takes its metadata off and shrinks it to a long edge of 1568 px before it is sent; at most 100 a call. A picture on another role, one that cannot be read, or one sent to a connection that cannot see is refused (bad_request). Ask first with ctx.llm.canSee(db, { profileId, profileSetting, chatId }), which names the connection the same way a call does and answers true, false, or null when the connection's model has not been checked (pictures are then sent, and a service that refuses them answers with its error).

A kind of connection of your own

The app's own kind of connection, openai, covers every OpenAI-compatible endpoint. A service with a shape of its own gets a kind from an extension: an adapter that turns the app's neutral request into that service's request and its answer back. The bundled Anthropic extension (extensions/bundled/anthropic/) is one, for Anthropic's Messages API; read it beside this section.

const { LlmError, classifyUpstream, boundedErrorText, networkError, readJsonBody, parseSse, translateParams } = ctx.llm.kit;
const PARAMS = { temperature: 'temperature', maxTokens: 'max_tokens' };
ctx.llm.registerAdapter({
    kind: 'my-service',                  // lowercase, 2 to 32 characters
    displayName: 'My Service',
    defaultBaseUrl: 'https://api.example.com',
    requiresBaseUrl: false,              // true when every connection must name its own endpoint
    capabilities: { streaming: true },   // a kind that cannot stream is refused for chat generation
    paramTable: PARAMS,
    async chat(profile, req, signal) { /* ... */ return { content, finishReason, usage }; },
    async *chatStream(profile, req, signal) { /* yield { text } or { reasoning }, then { done: { finishReason } } */ },
    async listModels(profile, signal) { /* ... */ return [{ id, contextLength, vision }]; },
});
  • profile is the connection (base_url, api_key, model), req carries messages, model and params (the app's neutral names; translateParams(req.params, PARAMS, body) writes the ones your table names onto your request body). Build the adapter from the kit: classifyUpstream(resp, await boundedErrorText(resp)) for an answer that is not ok, networkError(e) for a fetch that failed, readJsonBody(resp, signal) for an answer's body (null when it is not JSON; one past 8 MB is read no further and answers an upstream error; a read your signal stopped throws the abort, so a cancel or a timeout is never an empty reply), parseSse(resp.body, signal) for a stream's frames (a connection that drops part way is a network error, retried when no frame came before it), and new LlmError(kind, message) for anything else, so every failure is one of the app's error kinds. An answer with no body you recognize is new LlmError('upstream', 'unrecognized response shape'), never an empty reply or an empty model list. The app's retries, timeouts and cancelling wrap your methods; they never need their own.
  • req.messages is the conversation as the app builds it: system, user and assistant turns in the order the prompt places them. It can open or end on the model's turn, hold an empty turn, carry system text after the first turn, or be system text alone, and your adapter hands your service a conversation it accepts (the Anthropic adapter shows one way).
  • A user's turn can carry pictures, images: [{ mediaType, data, width, height }] beside its content, already shrunk to a long edge of at most 1568 px, upright, JPEG or PNG, data in base64. Declare capabilities: { vision: true } and put them in your service's image shape; a kind without it is never handed one, since the app refuses the call first. A turn with pictures can have empty text. Where your service's model list says whether a model sees pictures, answer it as vision (true or false) on that model, and leave it out where the list says nothing: the connection editor fills its Sees pictures field from it.
  • A stream ends at your service's end marker: stop reading there, since a service may hold its socket open after it. A body that ends without the marker was cut off, so throw new LlmError('network', message, { retryable: true }) rather than yielding done: a cut-off reply is then kept as interrupted, never as finished. A body in which nothing read as a frame (a page from a wrong address) is no cut-off reply: throw new LlmError('upstream', 'unrecognized response shape') there, as the call does for that page unstreamed. An error your service reports inside a stream is thrown like any other; classify it as the status your service gives that error outside a stream (classifyUpstream({ status }, text)), and one that arrives before the first word and would be retried at that status (an overload, a rate limit) is retried.
  • The kind is each account's, as everything else your extension adds: an account that switched your extension on sees it in the connection list (GET /api/llm/kinds, which names your extension beside it) and can make connections of it; an account without it has no such kind, and a connection of it made while it was on fails, naming its kind, until the extension is back on.
  • A kind the app ships or another extension holds, or an adapter missing chat, chatStream or listModels, fails your server half at the mount with the reason. Each call is counted in the inspector as kind <kind> chat, kind <kind> stream or kind <kind> models.

Background jobs

router.post('/tag-all', (req, res) => {
    const { id } = ctx.jobs.run(req.db, {
        key: 'tag-all',
        label: 'Tagging the library',
        run: async ({ db, user, signal, progress }) => {
            const ids = untagged(db);
            for (let i = 0; i < ids.length && !signal.aborted; i++) {
                await tagOne(db, ids[i], signal);
                progress({ done: i + 1, total: ids.length });
            }
        },
    });
    res.json({ id });
});

ctx.jobs.schedule({ id: 'tidy', every: 24 * 60 * 60 * 1000, label: 'Nightly tidy', run: async ({ db }) => tidy(db) });
  • run queues work for the account whose library db is and answers its { id } at once. Your jobs for one account run one at a time, in the order queued; another account's, and another extension's, run beside them.
  • The account sees its jobs in the notifications center, each by its label, your extension's name and its progress, a failure in your error's words; from there it cancels a job, dismisses a failure and clears the finished.
  • A key already queued and not yet started takes the new label and run in its place, so a burst of requests queues one job. A job with that key already running is left to finish, and the new one queues behind it.
  • progress({ done, total, note }) records how far the job has come: done of total draws the bar, and note says what the job is doing now. The notifications center reads it every two seconds while a job is in flight. GET /api/extensions/jobs lists the account's jobs, every extension's (the running, the queued with their places, the last finished), and a job's start and end reach the account's change feed as an extensionJob record.
  • ctx.jobs.list(db) answers your own jobs for that account in the same shape (each with its key and status: queued, running, done, failed or cancelled), never another extension's, even under the same key. Ask it whether a job for a key is already on its way rather than keeping your own note of what you queued: the account can cancel a queued job, and a note of yours never hears of it.
  • signal aborts when the account cancels the job (DELETE /api/extensions/jobs/{id}) or switches your extension off, when your extension is removed or its server half fails, or when an admin disables or deletes the account. A cancelled job ends at once and the next one starts; whatever your run does after is ignored, so stop at your next look at signal.aborted.
  • A throw ends the job as failed, in your error's words, and the next one starts. Every job that runs to an end is counted in the inspector as jobs.run, its failures as errors.
  • A job runs outside the request that queued it: what it writes reaches every tab, the one that asked included.
  • Jobs live in the server's memory, so a restart forgets the queued and the running. Work that has to survive one keeps its state in your own tables, where a schedule can pick it up again.
  • schedule queues run as a job for every account that has your extension on, every every milliseconds (at least one minute, at most 2147483647 ms, about 24 days), keyed so a tick never queues a second while the last still waits. The first tick comes one interval after the server starts serving, never at boot. An id is [a-z][a-z0-9-]{0,31}, unique among your schedules; an every outside those bounds or a second use of an id fails your server half at registration.
  • run and list refuse the server's own library and a library whose account has since switched your extension off, where you call them. They refuse an account an admin disabled or deleted too, naming the account, and your listeners hear nothing of it. ctx.llm refuses all three alike.

Server events

ctx.events.on(async (records, { db, user }) => {
    for (const r of records) {
        if (r.entity === 'message' && r.op === 'created') { /* ... */ }
    }
});

Your listener is handed each committed batch of changes to the library of an account that switched your extension on, once the write that made them has returned, as frozen records (the same records the change feed delivers to the account's tabs). It may be async; a throw or a rejection is caught and logged. What it writes through ctx.stores reaches every tab of that account, the one whose write set it off included.

A branch of a chat (the reader branching at a message) is a chat created record carrying branchOf: { chatId, seq, messageId }: the chat it came from and the message it ends on, by its place and its id. The branch holds that chat's messages up to seq at the same seqs (as new messages with ids of their own), and its metadata, lorebooks, persona and wallpaper; rows of your own keyed to the chat do not come with it unless you copy them, as the bundled Memory and Trackers do.

Ship a package.json containing { "type": "module" } at your extension root (or name the entry .mjs): the data directory can live anywhere on the host, and without one of those Node treats a bare .js file as CommonJS and refuses the export syntax.

Server halves load at boot only: installing or updating an extension with a server half shows "server: restart to apply" until the server restarts (an update answers { restart: true }, and the half keeps running the code it booted with until then). Removing an extension stops its routes at once: they answer 404 without a restart. A throwing register or a failed import marks the half as failed in the inventory with the reason, logs loudly, and never aborts the boot; the client half still loads. A requires.api newer than the server speaks makes the whole row broken instead, so neither half loads (see "Anatomy").

The trust model applies with full force here: a server half is code running inside the server process, for every user, with no fence around it. Its routes, guards and listeners serve only the accounts that switched it on, but register runs once at boot for the whole server, so work your half starts on its own (a timer, a cache warm-up) runs whoever switched it on.

Failure behavior

A throwing init (or a failed import) marks the extension as failed in Settings > Extensions, logs the error to the console, and never takes the app or other extensions down with it; whatever it registered before it failed is removed, your dispose() runs (when init ran) for what it did outside ctx, and its stylesheet goes with it. Server halves fail the same way: recorded, loud, contained.

Every call the app makes into your code (an action's run and when, a window's hooks, an event listener, a wrapper, a command of yours, init itself, a store guard and a server event listener) is contained and timed. Settings > Extensions has an Inspect button on each extension loaded in that tab or with a server half that registered something: everything it registered, and for each call the runs, the median and 95th-percentile times, the total, and the failures with the last error, since it loaded in that tab, or for the server's calls, since the server started, for your own account only (with a guard's refusals counted beside its failures).

Example

A tour of the api ships with the app as extensions/bundled/hello-world/: each door shown once and small, its files carrying the published types (see "Types for your editor"). It shows every door but four, which have references of their own: a provider (the RisuAI Realm), a kind of connection (the Anthropic extension), an overlay of a shape of your own (the Chat Timeline) and an answer read from your server half as it streams (ctx.server.stream, Next Beat), and it keeps its files through ctx.files rather than the bare paths ctx.dataDir() and ctx.userDataDir(req). Its conversation hooks, its lore book and its prompt block each wait behind a setting that starts off. It is bundled but off by default ("defaultOff": true in its manifest, which makes a bundled extension opt-in like an install); switch it on in Settings > Extensions to try the api, and read its source beside this page. Its test, extensions/bundled/hello-world/test/hello-world.test.mjs, is the kit's worked example (see "Testing your extension").

Provider extensions

An extension can be a full character source. Set "provider": true in the manifest and register a ProviderBase instance from your client entry:

import myProvider from './my-provider.js';

export default {
    async init(ctx) {
        await ctx.providers.register(myProvider, {
            name: 'My Source',
            extNamespace: 'mysource',
            searchPrefixes: ['mysource', 'ms'],
        });
    },
};

The namespace is declared in manifest.json, and it is optional: without it, the namespace is your extension id. Your provider's id must be its namespace (mysource here); register refuses a provider named otherwise.

{ "provider": true, "extNamespace": "mysource" }
  • The namespace is validated when the extension is listed: lowercase [a-z0-9_-], starting with a letter or digit, at most 32 chars. An illegal one (an id longer than 32 chars with no explicit extNamespace, say) makes the extension a broken row that names the problem, and an install carrying one is refused. So is a namespace that is someone else's key: a built-in provider's (chub, wyvern and the rest), one the app owns in a card (world, depth_prompt, fav, cl and its other identity and state keys), or one card tools write for their own data (risuai, talkativeness, regex_scripts, sd_character_prompt), since every card carrying that key would have its data turned into your link. Of two extensions declaring one namespace, the later (by id) is refused. A namespace the app has retired (a source it does not ship, whose links a library may still hold) is open to one extension: claim it and your provider owns the links a library already holds under it, which read as yours while the extension is on and wait intact while it is off.
  • The declaration makes the server treat your links like first-party ones: a card's data.extensions.<namespace> is stored as a provider link, which is what powers exact in-library detection and the linking surfaces. Choose the name with care: a registered namespace is a permanent, server-wide commitment. It keeps routing writes to link rows even after your extension is removed, so the data users created through it stays consistent and reconnects in full if the extension returns.
  • The second argument to register is the provider's manifest row, the same declaration the built-in providers carry, so registration is the whole job: your search operators (ms:yes), the advanced-filter entry, the mobile avatar tap and the Online selector all pick the row up, and the selector and the grid refresh so a late registration is visible at once. Extension providers rank after the built-ins. A registration whose init throws rolls back completely, and switching the extension off takes the provider and its row back out (the Online tab moves to another provider if yours was showing).
  • A settings surface (credentials, feature toggles) is ctx.ui.addProviderSettingsSection, called right after providers.register: the section appears at your provider's place in Settings > Online, dressed like the built-in ones, and exists only while your extension is loaded.

Build the provider itself the way the built-ins are built:

  • Extend ProviderBase and BrowseView, imported absolutely (/modules/providers/provider-interface.js and /modules/providers/browse-view.js; these resolve to the page's own modules whether the page is served by its server or apart). Keep API calls in an -api.js, and put third-party text through the sanitizer paths (ctx.CoreAPI.safePurify with BROWSE_PURIFY_CONFIG from /modules/providers/provider-utils.js).
  • An import ends in one of provider-utils' two funnels: importFromPng for a card you built and a picture you downloaded, or importFromStage for a card file your server half staged (ctx.imports.stage), whose card you amend and commit. Both fit the card the same way (the name's length, the author's Import Rules) and answer the same result.
  • Your preview's import button calls BrowseView's importFromPreview({ button, hit, provider, id, data, closePreview, probe, dialog }), which runs the library's duplicate check and its dialog (Skip, Import Anyway, or Replace, which writes the card onto the character it matched), imports through character.import and lights the card. A prepare() in place of id, data, probe and dialog computes them when the button is pressed, and a ready() answers id, data and options once the dialog has answered, for what may land while it is open (a detail your preview is still fetching).
  • With the reader's "Import in the background" setting on (the default) the import joins the background queue every browse view shares, one import per site at a time, and your preview closes; for its button and grid card to say so, your card template adds view.importingBadge(row) to the card's badges and your preview hands its row to view.trackPreview(restamp, row) when it opens.
  • Override galleryUrlFor(result) when your site has a gallery page for the summary to offer. Name a row's key in hitLinkKey(row) and ask view.isHitInLibrary(row) whether the library links it (never the base's underscore members), read the reader's exclude tags for your provider with view.persistentExcludeTags(), and spread libraryFeatureRows(idPrefix, () => view, (row) => view.isHitPossibleMatch(row)) from /modules/providers/browse-view.js into your Features table for its Hide Owned and Hide Possible Matches rows (isHitPossibleMatch reads the badge the grid shows for the row).
  • The nine built-in provider trios under app/modules/providers/ are the reference to copy: browse, search, link and import client code, and where the upstream needs one, a server half (session handling, a hardened proxy via ctx.helpers.guardProxyTarget and friends) rides along in the same package. The bundled RisuAI Realm is the reference for a provider shipped as an extension, except for its row: it claims the one the app declares for it, so it calls providers.register with the provider alone, where yours passes its row as the second argument.

Bundled extensions

Extensions in the application's own extensions/bundled/ folder ship with the app: they appear in Settings > Extensions in their own Bundled group under the server's installs, and can be switched on and off per user but not removed or updated through the UI (they update with the app). A bundled extension runs for every user until they switch it off, unless its manifest says "defaultOff": true, which makes it opt-in like an install. An installed extension cannot take a bundled extension's id.

Fourteen ship today, each off until an account switches it on. Each is a reference for one kind of extension; read its source beside the section it names:

  • Hello World (hello-world): the tour, each door shown once and small (see "Example"). Start here.
  • Ratings and Notes (ratings-notes): data an extension owns. A table of its own, writes that report, all three query doors, a grid chip, a details tab and a verb on a selection. Read it before storing data of your own.
  • Chat Timeline (chat-timeline): a server half that answers a read the client would otherwise assemble from every transcript; a window in the conversation and an overlay of its own. Every chat with a character drawn as one branching tree.
  • Auto-tagger (auto-tagger): background work with the account's own model (ctx.jobs, ctx.llm), its answers kept as suggestions the client applies through tags.apply.
  • Memory (memory): work on a chat with the account's own model: a running summary of each chat kept in a table, placed by a prompt block and a macro, edited in a window.
  • Translator (translator): the conversation's hooks (message.render, message.enhance, composer.send), with a translation stored on the message beside a digest of what it translated.
  • Library Stats (library-stats): a view of your own (shell.view), loaded on first use.
  • Lore Search (lore-search): lore. A search index of its own over every book, a dialog, a lore action, and lore.candidates adding entries from books a chat does not use.
  • RisuAI Realm (risu-realm): a provider. Browse, preview and import from the Realm, the server half downloading the character's file into a stage (ctx.imports.stage) and the client committing it through importFromStage.
  • Trackers (trackers), inspired by kaldigo's Tracker for SillyTavern: state a chat keeps per turn, in tables keyed onto the message, read into the prompt as of the message a reply follows (lastSeq), with a window and an editor.
  • Next Beat (next-beat): the composer's doors, inspired by mattjaybe's Pathweaver for SillyTavern (MIT). Buttons above the composer whose menus open beside their anchor, suggestions handed to chat.compose and chat.send, a route whose answer streams a line a suggestion (read with ctx.server.stream and stopped by its signal), buttons whose when follows a setting, and a prompt block answering a note kept in the chat's slot only on the build it is due. A setting moves its verbs into a window of its own: the buttons' when and the window's when read it, and one renderer per surface draws into either a dialog or the window, which closes itself through its context's close on a phone once a suggestion is inserted or sent.
  • Story Beats (story-beats): a chat's story steered toward moments planned ahead, without holding a reply up. Beats kept in the chat's slot (ctx.chatSlot) and placed by a prompt block where the settings say, a director that reads the chat after the model's replies (a server event and ctx.jobs, ctx.llm), its log in a table of its own, a window with a planner, and commands other extensions run (story-beats.next, .status, .skip, .force).
  • Midnight theme (theme-midnight): a theme that gives the whole app a new look, on the public doors alone. Its sheet maps the app's theme tokens onto palette properties its client writes on the page's root, and restyles the surfaces at the app's own weight, so the reader's theme settings and custom CSS still win. It changes the structure too: a rail of views down the left edge on a wide desktop, portraits in arched windows with their names on a plate, a character's replies set on the night under their name with the reader's turns in glass capsules, and a character's details opening on a band of their portrait's light (it follows the character.open command to know which). Each of these, the display face, the corners and the motion is a choice in its settings section that applies at once, written as a class on the page's root. Four palettes and an Auto palette that follows the time of day; an accent it sets only while the reader has picked none; one canvas drawing a sky behind everything, at thirty frames a second or fewer, still under reduced motion and resting while the tab is hidden; a conversation tinted from its character's portrait at a readable contrast; palettes of the reader's own kept as files of their account (ctx.files.user) and edited in its settings section with a live preview; two commands (theme-midnight.next, theme-midnight.palette) and a verb in the app menu.
  • Σ theme (theme-sigma): the app's own design finished, on the same public doors as Midnight and built the same way (palette properties on the root, every rule at the app's own weight), so the two read as siblings. Where Midnight builds another world, Σ takes the app's own newest surface (the chat windows: Inter, hairline edges lit along the top, quiet depth) as its spec and carries it to every other surface, reaching the app's own Inter through --font-stack-ui rather than shipping a face; the views sit in a segmented bar or a rail. Its palettes are pairs of colours on near-neutral inks, with the accent, the primary verb's fill and a conversation's voices held readable on the brightest ground a reply meets. One canvas drifts soft pools of the palette's light behind everything, under the same frame and motion limits as Midnight's sky. It dresses the app's own Search everything (.cl-palette) in its material and adds nothing to it. Two commands (theme-sigma.next, theme-sigma.palette) and one verb in the app menu. A theme of your own can still be a stylesheet alone: a manifest with a style entry, and a sheet of the tokens app/library.css declares at :root.
  • Anthropic (anthropic): a kind of connection, a server half alone that registers an adapter for Anthropic's Messages API (see "A kind of connection of your own").
